| D-263 | 2026-08-23 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-264](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **A chooser is told two roles: one for the open list, one for the closed field.** The owner, describing the selection field — *I name a node and can then choose its children, as with the constants* — asked for the **text type to be selectable** there: the short sign for prefixes, the symbol for `Ohm`. And he named the trick from the previous project: *I had a list field that held **symbol plus description**, because that is nicer — you pick symbol plus description, and the field is fairly narrow, so afterwards you only see the symbol.* ⚠️ **That separates two things the concept had run together:** choosing needs enough text to **tell apart** — `Ω — Ohm`, `k — Kilo` — while the closed field needs only enough to **recognise** — `Ω`. Two needs, two roles, both set as ordinary settings ([D-259](#)). **The role for the first already exists and had never been explained:** `select` from the seeded set ([D-196](#)) — the field standing beside `Symbol` on the legacy detail screen. ⚠️ **One addition, so nobody maintains a third text:** the open list is **composed by default** — symbol plus name — rather than stored, or `Ohm` would sit in two places and the list would lag behind an edited description. Whoever wants a bespoke list text enters `select` explicitly and it wins. [D-032](#) again: a derived default with a deliberate override. | `agreed`, the `select` half superseded by [D-264](#) | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [40 I18n](40-i18n.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | extends [D-259](#) |

| D-264 | 2026-08-23 | **A renderer is told a **pattern** of roles and fixed characters, not a single role. `select` leaves the seeded set. Supersedes that half of [D-263](#).** The owner, thinking his own trick through again: *maybe a separate list field is silly. Perhaps it is better simply to say what is shown in this list field — symbol, dash, explanation. Short text. That avoids the extra field and makes us more flexible.* ⚠️ **It generalises [D-259](#):** instead of one role, a renderer gets **several plus fixed characters** — `symbol` – `form`, or just `table`, or `form` (`symbol`). A stored list text becomes unnecessary, and combinations nobody has thought of yet come free. **What is lost:** a list text that cannot be built from existing roles. That is smaller than it sounds, because literals are allowed — `Ω (SI)` is `symbol` plus a literal — and what remains is only *this thing should be called something completely different in lists*, for which the owner's own answer is the right one: *if they are to be called differently, then I have to change the text.* **A hard limit, so it does not become a small language:** **roles and fixed characters, nothing else.** No conditions, no loops, no formatting. The moment someone wants *if there is no symbol, then…* they are describing the fallback chain ([D-020](#)), which is where it belongs. **`select` therefore leaves the seeded set of [D-196](#)** — a role read off the legacy screen this morning without anyone knowing what it was for. It was the frozen answer to exactly this question, and the pattern is the better one. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [40 I18n](40-i18n.md) | supersedes part of [D-263](#), [D-196](#) |

| D-265 | 2026-08-23 | **A table footer aggregate is a block setting, and it totals what is shown — not what exists.** The code sweep found a footer band with a per-column aggregate (`none · text · sum · avg · min · max · count`) and flagged it as missing; the owner placed it: *the footer was meant for the block in Gutenberg, so that sums can be made there — a setting of the block.* ⚠️ **That resolves a tension rather than adding a feature.** A **computed attribute** ([D-045](#)) says *the total of **this parts list*** — a fact about the thing, available everywhere and searchable. A **footer** says *the total of **this table*** — of what is on the screen, which may be filtered or a fragment. Both are wanted, and they answer different questions: when a table shows part of something, a person looking at it almost always wants the total **of that part**. **It therefore falls under [D-257](#)** — page-local, stored in the post, with the known cost that nothing can query it. Harmless here, because a column total is not something a model change would need to find again. **Closes one of the 18 *missing* rows** of [harvest 04](_harvest/04-legacy-code-inspiration.md). | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | places a legacy finding |

| D-266 | 2026-08-23 | **An override can be reset to *inherited*, and that is not the same as storing an empty value.** Taken from the legacy code at the owner's instruction (*that we should adopt*): `class-settings-walk.php` — *reset deletes the delta key; empty uses the type list*. ⚠️ **[D-015](#) makes overrides sparse but never said how one is removed, which is exactly where sparse schemes fail.** Two distinct acts: **reset** makes the key **disappear**, so the value is inherited again **and future changes at the origin reach here once more**; **set to empty** leaves the key in place holding nothing, so this use site deliberately has no value **and changes at the origin do not reach it**. Losing that distinction loses the ability to say *here there should deliberately be nothing* — and it is noticed only when a change at the origin surfaces somewhere nobody wanted it. **Consequences for the interface:** *inherited* and *empty here* must be **visibly different** — the legacy detail screen's `override` badge is exactly that marker — and returning to inherited is an **explicit action**, not the side effect of clearing a field. **It applies to labels as much as to settings**, since [D-015](#) governs both, and it is the mechanism by which [D-223](#)'s *automatic is a default, never a fact* is actually revoked. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | fills a gap in [D-015](#) |

| D-267 | 2026-08-23 | **The `id` stays as it is. The legacy running number splits into three things, and only one of them is missing.** The owner, on the `seq` column the code sweep found: *in the old project the id was simply there to identify a node — I think we have that. We could use a running number as the id, but I see no advantage in changing it.* So [D-055](#) and [D-022](#) stand untouched. **What the finding really contained:** (1) **the id** — unchanged; (2) **numbers with a rule** — article number, EAN, order number — *those probably need a number circle, which would be a type of its own*; (3) **row numbers** — *maybe only needed in the block*. Three different things that the single legacy column had run together, which is why it looked like one gap. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | places a legacy finding |
| D-268 | 2026-08-23 | **A number circle is a type of its own, and it is the first thing in the model whose state changes without anyone editing it.** For article numbers, EANs and order numbers a rule is needed — prefix, starting value, step, width — so the number circle is a **node carrying that rule and its current position**, and an attribute is told to draw its value from it. ⚠️ **The novelty is worth naming:** every draw advances it, so it is the first construct here whose stored state moves **without a user changing anything**. Everything else in this concept changes only because a person did something, which is what makes the changelog ([D-061](#)) readable as a history of intentions. **Not decided here**, and deliberately: whether draws may leave **gaps** when a record is deleted. For an article number a gap is nothing; for an invoice number it can be a legal matter, and that question belongs with the first real invoice, not with the mechanism. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | from the legacy sweep |
| D-269 | 2026-08-23 | **A row number is a reading aid, never an identifier.** 1, 2, 3 down a table — not stored, computed from the display, following whatever order the table is in. ⚠️ **Sort the table differently and row 3 is a different row**, which is precisely why it cannot be referred to. **The distinction matters in the owner's own example:** `Pos. 3` in a parts list **does** mean something to a person, and something that means something must be a **real attribute** with a value of its own — not the display's counter. Where a position number is meaningful it is modelled; where it is only orientation it is drawn. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | with [D-267](#) |

| D-270 | 2026-08-23 | **Row numbers are switched on in the block, and off by default.** Following [D-269](#), the counter is a reading aid computed from the display, so whether it appears is a page decision like any other visibility ([D-253](#)) — *row numbers in the block, activate*. Off by default because a numbered column costs width and most tables are read without one; where a long list needs orientation it is one switch. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | with [D-269](#) |
| D-271 | 2026-08-23 | **A parking lot, with a rule that keeps it from becoming a graveyard.** The owner: *plan the number circle for a later release — make a parking lot, nice to have, and when we need it we fetch it back.* It lives in [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) beside the release sections, and it is a **third** category: Release 2 holds what is placed, the deferral section holds what waits on a **named event**, and the parking lot holds what is **wanted but not needed** and waits on nothing. **Each entry carries two things** — what it is, and **what would make us want it**; not a trigger that fires by itself, but the situation in which it becomes attractive. ⚠️ **And parking is explicitly not a promise:** an entry may be struck without anyone explaining why. Without that, a parking lot accumulates every idea anybody ever had and nobody dares remove one. **First entry: the number circle** ([D-268](#)). | `agreed` | [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) | — |

| D-272 | 2026-08-23 | **The parking lot is walked at every release planning.** The owner: *the idea would be to look at the parking lot for each release and see what we want to implement.* ⚠️ **That is what gives the lot its value; without a rhythm such a list rots.** Entries accumulate, nobody reads them, and eventually nobody dares strike one. A fixed moment does three things at once: the list gets **read**, the *what would make us want it* column is actually **tested** — *is that true now?* — and there is a natural occasion to **strike**. Something looked at three times and not wanted three times is probably not wanted. It also gives release planning a source of scope that is neither invention nor backlog. | `agreed` | [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) | extends [D-271](#) |

| D-273 | 2026-08-23 | **One tree for all subject areas. No separate model roots, no projects.** The legacy sweep found that the concept never decided how recipes, PC hardware and ESP projects ([D-175](#)) coexist in one installation, and that the legacy had a `Project` construct with a `start_node`. The owner: *all of this is on my website, and yes I want it at the same time — it makes no sense to create separate trees or projects for it.* So they are ordinary branches under `Model`, side by side, and the single model root of the resolution chain ([D-079](#)) stands unchanged. ⚠️ **Sharing is the point, not merely convenient:** recipes and hardware both use `Gramm`, `Anzahl`, `Medium`. Separate trees would hold each of those **more than once**, leaving the choice between maintaining them in several places and letting them drift — the duplicated fact this concept refuses everywhere else. **The price, named:** what is shared acts everywhere. Narrowing `Gramm` ([D-221](#)) or renaming it touches recipes **and** hardware. Usually that is exactly what is wanted, but it is the trade. **And the top level of `Model` simply *is* the list of subject areas** — which is what the legacy *hide root node* setting displays. That switch was already the right answer; the question was missing beside it. **Closes one of the 11 *missing* rows** of [harvest 03](_harvest/03-legacy-inspiration.md). | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | places a legacy finding |

| D-274 | 2026-08-23 | **A unit carries its factor to the reference unit of its parent. Closes a gap in [D-039](#).** The legacy sweep found that the concept settles only the **prefix** axis: [D-039](#) says a length *changes its prefix* and a currency *changes its unit* — and **inch falls between them**, being a length that is not a prefixed metre. The owner confirmed the need: *conversion would be nice; now and then there are units given in inches, and it would be good to convert them into metric.* ⚠️ **No new construct is needed.** Inch and metre are both lengths, so they share a **parent node**, and by [D-041](#) that parent **is** their type. The *dimension* is therefore already present and is called inheritance. **Each unit carries its factor to the parent's reference unit**, so inch → millimetre is a multiplication through the shared parent, and the same rule carries foot, mile, ounce and pound. **Two limits, stated rather than discovered later:** temperature needs **more than a factor** — °C → °F is `×1.8 + 32`, an **offset** — so the rule is factor **and** offset, or the commonest conversion of all falls outside it. And whatever is neither factor nor offset is a **converter** ([D-219](#)): wire gauge to cross-section is a table, not a calculation. The rule covers the linear case and hands the rest over honestly instead of bending. ⚠️ **What is decided here is the shape, not the feature** — *display this in metric* is parked ([D-271](#)). The shape is decided now because it is what would otherwise be found out too late. **Closes one of the 11 *missing* rows** of [harvest 03](_harvest/03-legacy-inspiration.md). | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | closes a gap in [D-039](#) |

| D-275 | 2026-08-23 | **Converting between peer units moves from the parking lot into Release 2.** The owner, an hour after it was parked: *I would like to take the conversion into release 2.* ⚠️ **The distinction from [D-274](#) holds and matters:** the **shape** is Release 1 — a unit carries its factor, and where needed its offset, to the reference unit of its parent, so the model can *express* a conversion from the first day. What moves to Release 2 is the **doing**: a converter and a renderer that show a value in a unit other than the one it was entered in. Deciding the shape early and the behaviour late is what keeps the data from having to be migrated when the behaviour arrives. **Incidentally the first demonstration that the parking lot works** ([D-271](#), [D-272](#)) — an entry parked in the afternoon and placed the same evening, which is the movement the lot exists to make possible in both directions. | `agreed` | [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) | places [D-274](#) |

| D-276 | 2026-08-23 | **There is no second audience. The registries are internal.** The legacy sweep raised it as a gap: the old product appeared to exist so that **host plugins** — `wp-electronic-parts` — could consume it, with hooks, side panels and a documented API, while the concept has registries and a boundary but no decision on whether outside code may register into them. ⚠️ **The owner dissolved it:** *`wp-electronic-parts` was the predecessor of the predecessor. I renamed it at some point — so it is not an external plugin.* The legacy documentation was not wrong, it was **stale**: it described a relationship between two plugins that had since become one plugin with two names. **What follows:** registries may change **whenever** they need to, with no stable public contract, no interface versioning, and no question about what happens when foreign code returns nonsense. ⚠️ **Public interfaces are expensive** — from the day someone else registers, your own contract is frozen — and that is a constraint to take on only for a reason. There is none here. **What stands is the boundary** ([D-170](#), [D-171](#)), but for the reason it was decided for: **portability**, so a second boundary can be set beside the first. That remains right even though nobody calls in from outside. **Closes one of the 11 *missing* rows** of [harvest 03](_harvest/03-legacy-inspiration.md). | `agreed` | [00 Vision and scope](00-vision-and-scope.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | places a legacy finding |

| D-277 | 2026-08-23 | **Renderer and converter form a pair. The converter belongs to the list entry, not to the node.** Resolves the contradiction the second renderer catch-up raised: [D-219](#) fixed *exactly one converter in effect* by analogy — *the same shape [D-217](#) gives renderers* — and [D-236](#) has since made that shape a **list**. **The concrete case forces it:** a number and a colour code shown side by side need **two different mappings at the same moment** — the number a plain converter or none, the rings the ring converter. So a list entry is **a renderer together with its converter**, and [D-219](#)'s sentence becomes precise rather than wrong: **one converter per rendering**, which was always the true statement — *one per node* held only while there was one renderer. The owner's own restatement: *renderer and converter always appear as a pair.* ⚠️ **The converter half may be empty** — a plain number renderer needs none. **And in most cases nothing is chosen:** by [D-220](#) a renderer declares which result shape it can draw and the eligible converters are those producing it, so where one remains it is simply used. A choice arises only where a type carries **two** converters of the same shape — two shorthand notations, say. ⚠️ **The owner asked whether one renderer might need **several** converters, and left it open: *that has not shown itself so far, and if the converter forms a binding with the renderer then pairing them is probably still right.* Searched for a counter-example and found none that holds — the nearest candidate is [D-149](#)'s renderer with two coupled controls, but that uses **one** converter **twice**, once to draw and once to read, which is one mapping in two directions rather than two mappings. Recorded so nobody has to derive it again; if a real case appears, the pair becomes a small list and nothing else changes. **A small shift, not a new construct:** the converter moves one level down, to where it always belonged. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | refines [D-219](#) after [D-236](#) |

| D-278 | 2026-08-23 | **The *level* is retired. What it did is purpose, depth and visibility — all of which already exist. Resolves the last contradiction.** A never-decided passage gave the preview a **level** switch — admin / block / front end — while [D-231](#) says the preview shows the front end and may deviate only in size. ⚠️ **The owner's doubt went further than my proposal to reconcile them:** *I believe Gutenberg and the back-end view look the same. And there is no admin render function — in the admin there is really only the preview of the data.* **He is right, and it dissolves the concept:** no edit controls in the front end is the **purpose** ([D-217](#)); more nesting in the admin is **depth**, a setting the legacy screen itself made overridable per block; fewer fields is **visibility**, decided by the block ([D-253](#)). Three settings that often travel together, under a collective name that has no appearance of its own. **So [D-231](#) stands unchanged and the switch is struck rather than saved.** ⚠️ *A convenience switch that flips those three settings at once can be built later as a preset — but then it is convenience, not a term in the model.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | retires the level; upholds [D-231](#) |
| D-279 | 2026-08-23 | **Preview what you cannot see while configuring. The search view belongs in it; the tree row does not.** The owner named two further surfaces and judged them differently: *one is rendering the node in the tree — I think we do not need that in the preview. And the third leg is the search; the question is whether we want the search view in the preview.* **The rule underneath both answers:** a preview exists for what is **elsewhere**. **The tree row needs none because it is already on screen** — set the icon or the label pattern and the row beside you changes; a preview of what you are looking at is a second picture of the same thing. **The search view needs one because it is configurable and invisible from here:** which renderer serves search, which operators are offered, whether the attribute is searchable at all through `supports()` ([D-217](#)). ⚠️ **What can be configured must be previewable, or it is configured blind.** **And search is a purpose, not a level** ([D-278](#)), so the preview has **three** pictures — display, edit, search — all from a term the concept already has. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | with [D-278](#) |

| D-280 | 2026-08-23 | **A node search sits above the tree and inside every chooser.** The owner: *yes — one could show a filter at the top. But it was also meant for the tree chooser, where the old system had a search field.* Two places, one behaviour: type, see the matches, see how many. At a few hundred nodes it stops being a convenience. Distinct from record search ([D-167](#)), which looks for **data**; this looks for **model**. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-281 | 2026-08-23 | **A duplicate edge is refused, and the name is part of what makes it a duplicate.** The owner asked what *duplicate* could even mean: *in an attribute I can perfectly well send two edges to `int` and give them different names — almost the same edge, but different purposes.* ⚠️ **Exactly, and that is why the legacy check keyed on From + kind + To **and name**.** `Breite` and `Höhe` both reach `int` and are two different things. Only an edge identical **including its name** is refused, and a nameless edge duplicating another nameless one likewise — a second identical connection carries no information. ⚠️ *The owner's own reservation, recorded: he is not sure how often anyone would actually create a duplicate edge. The check is cheap and the message is clear, so it stays — but it is a guard, not a feature.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-282 | 2026-08-23 | **An unchanged save does not raise the version.** Opening a record, looking at it and saving leaves the version where it was; only changed values raise it. ⚠️ **This matters more than it looks, because [D-210](#) hangs migration on the version:** if every save counted as a change, the changelog would fill with events in which nothing happened, and *which records were touched by this model change* would stop being answerable. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [70 Migration](70-migration.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-283 | 2026-08-23 | **Nobody loses their place. This is craft, not a feature.** The owner's framing, and it changes where the requirement belongs: *remembering where you are, in the chooser and in the tree, is mainly for when you save — otherwise you have lost your place, either the focus on the page or the node. That should be avoided as far as possible. It is decent programming and design rather than a feature.* So: after saving, the selection and the focus stay where they were. **And restoring the expanded state across a reload is a bonus on top** — ids that no longer exist are simply dropped. Written down because *decent design* is exactly the kind of requirement that is never written down and therefore never built. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-284 | 2026-08-23 | **Keyboard and screen-reader behaviour is a baseline requirement.** ⚠️ *The owner had no opinion — it means nothing to him — so this is my call.* Tree rows carry their expanded and selected state, the chooser is a labelled list, `Escape` closes it and returns focus to what opened it. **It is written down now because it cannot be retrofitted cheaply:** it is a property of every control, so adding it later means touching every control, while building it in costs almost nothing. | `agreed` (my call, no owner opinion) | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-285 | 2026-08-23 | **A converter that cannot represent a value falls back to the canonical form and says so at the field.** Roman numerals cover `1..3999` and no negatives; beyond that the legacy simply returned the decimal. **The fallback is right** — refusing to draw would hide the value entirely — and the owner added what makes it honest: *a small circle with an exclamation mark and a note at the field, that this cannot be shown because it is greater than 4000.* ⚠️ **Without the marker the fallback lies quietly:** the reader sees a decimal where they expected a notation and cannot tell whether the converter is off, broken, or out of range. Same family as [D-147](#)'s *not computable* — an inability is shown, not swallowed. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-286 | 2026-08-23 | **The compact renderer factors out a shared unit and names the members in the label.** `10.5/20/5 mm` rather than `10.5mm/20mm/5mm`, and the label reads `Abmessung (L/B/H)`. Both apply **only** where every member shares one type and that type carries a unit — otherwise the factoring would be a lie. Separator configurable, default `/`. The owner wants it kept for the compact renderer ([D-245](#)). | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-287 | 2026-08-23 | **Excluding a choice excludes its subtree; a media attribute restricts the kinds it accepts. Both are [D-221](#).** *All choices start enabled; unchecking a node excludes it **and its subtree*** — the allow-list of [D-221](#) with the obvious reading, since excluding a branch while keeping its children would mean the children are reachable through nothing. And a media attribute carries an allow-list of **MIME kinds**, which is the same construct applied to what [D-229](#) detects. ⚠️ The legacy warned *select at least one MIME kind — media fields do nothing until a kind is enabled*, which is [D-157](#)'s unsatisfiable model: an empty allow-list is a model conflict, caught where the narrowing happens. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-288 | 2026-08-23 | **An error blocks, a warning stays visible and does not.** The legacy envelope separated `blocking` from `warnings` — *saving with warnings is always allowed*. The owner: *an error blocks, always. A warning stays there, visible, and then you see that something is not yet as it should be.* ⚠️ **And he named the case that tests it:** while modelling one sometimes wants to save **through** an error, because something else has to be created first — *then you take the error out for a while*. That is the honest answer rather than a loophole: the block stays, and the way through is to relax the rule deliberately, not to click past it. **[V9](00-vision-and-scope.md) gains what it never said:** a complaint may be non-blocking. A validator therefore yields severity as well as a message and possibly a correction. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | from the legacy code sweep |
| D-289 | 2026-08-23 | **Uninstalling offers an export first, and deleting the data is a choice.** The legacy code had **no** activation hook, no deactivation hook and no uninstall — its table appeared lazily on first use, which **CD-6** forbids outright. The owner: *one option is to delete the data along with it. The second option is to export the data — that I would want to offer the user before uninstalling.* So removal asks rather than assumes, and the export is offered **before** anything is removed, when it is still possible. ⚠️ **Leaving the data behind must be a deliberate answer too**, not the default that happens when someone clicks past the dialogue: orphaned tables in a database nobody can attribute later are their own kind of mess. | `agreed` | [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy code sweep |

| D-290 | 2026-08-23 | **Sample values that appear together are coherent. Refines [D-240](#).** The legacy filled a preview from **one shared persona** — *Herbert Müller · herbert@home.de · Muster GmbH · Berlin* — rather than from independent per-field examples. ⚠️ **It looks cosmetic and is not.** A preview exists to show whether something **reads** ([D-231](#)), and three unrelated placeholders cannot show that: with a coherent set one sees at once whether the column is too narrow and whether the order makes sense, while *sample text · sample text* shows only that fields exist. So [D-240](#)'s samples stay on the type, and where several appear together they are **drawn from one coherent set**. ⚠️ **And the owner drew the boundary: coherence can only be shipped, never guessed.** For the **built-in** types we can lay down samples that fit together — that is our job and it is worth doing. For anything the **user** creates, the system knows nothing about what would fit, and coherence comes from his own **marked test data** ([D-241](#)) instead. Nothing tries to invent a relationship between fields it has never seen. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | refines [D-240](#) |
| D-291 | 2026-08-23 | **One date-and-time type with a precision setting. Stored in UTC — except a plain date, which has no timezone at all.** The legacy had three types, `date`, `time` and `datetime`, and had already considered folding them into one that is configured. **Yes, one type**, for the reason that has decided every such case today: one stored value, presentation configured. Three types would mean three sets of renderers, converters and validators for the same thing. **Timezones, never touched before:** stored in **UTC**, displayed in the **site's** timezone — the owner's own instinct, and his example names the only case where it really hurts: a reader in the USA should see that the post was written **at night**, not at their own time of day. ⚠️ **But the precision is not merely a display choice, and this is the trap:** a **plain date has no timezone**. A birthday is 14 March everywhere; store it as `14.03. 00:00 UTC` and it becomes **13 March** for everyone west of that. So *store the full value in the background* holds with one restriction — **a date stays a date and is never rotated through a timezone**; a point in time is. The precision therefore governs **meaning**, not only appearance. **The site timezone applies in both directions** — the owner: *UTC we can do, but I enter my data in GMT+1, in CET; please bear that in mind.* So input is read as site-local and stored as UTC; output is the reverse. He types and reads local, always. **The precisions, surveyed at his request** — *there are many kinds of date, birthday and perhaps age, but that is only years; have a look at what there is and take it in exemplarily*: **year** (year of publication, 1981 — his own `Year of Publication` on the ISA bus), **month** (a shelf life, `03/2027`), **date** (birthday, invoice date), **time of day** (opening at 18:00), **point in time** (created at, measured at). ⚠️ **Only the last is timezone-bearing.** ⚠️ **And one thing that looks like it belongs and does not: duration** — three hours, two days. That is not a point but a **quantity**, so it belongs to [D-274](#): time is a dimension with units that convert into one another. ⚠️ *The owner doubts the case arises often — recorded as his doubt, and as the reason to keep the rule to this one sentence rather than building timezone machinery around it.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | from the legacy code sweep |

| D-292 | 2026-08-23 | **There are roles, they are built in from the start, and an administrator may practically everything.** The owner: *there are roles and they should be built in. And of course there is the admin role, which may practically everything.* ⚠️ **This was the largest genuine gap left by the legacy sweep:** the concept had accumulated places that need an answer — building the model, switching on developer mode ([D-248](#), [D-122](#)), declaring a branch extensible and **judging** an addition ([D-204](#)), emptying the trash ([D-123](#)), uninstalling and deleting data ([D-289](#)) — while saying nothing about who may do any of it. **And [D-204](#) had already assumed roles without naming them:** a review in which everyone may judge their own addition is not a review. **Which roles exist and what each may do is not settled here** — that is a list to draw up, not a principle — but two things are: roles exist as a first-class concept rather than being retrofitted, and the administrator is the one who may everything. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | closes the largest legacy gap |
| D-293 | 2026-08-23 | **A field can take its default — or its restriction — from the record that encloses it.** The owner described it as an input aid: *on the parts list I say I assume everything is through-hole technology, and then when choosing parts I narrow it down accordingly.* **Two strengths, and they are two constructs the concept already has:** *mostly so* is a **default** that is preset and may be changed ([D-032](#)); *everything is so* is a **restriction** that cannot ([D-221](#)). ⚠️ **What is new is the source.** Every restriction so far is set in the **model** and stands still; this one is read from a **value in the enclosing record**, at the moment of entry. The addressing exists — relative paths ([D-045](#)) — so an attribute can say *take my default, or my permitted set, from there*. **The owner has no mechanism in mind** — *I have no idea yet how we could set that up* — and none is decided here beyond the shape. It is worth having: it turns *choose from four thousand parts* into *choose from the sixty that fit*, which is the difference between a usable form and one people avoid. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy sweep |
| D-294 | 2026-08-23 | **A mirrored file is fetched once on saving, refreshed only when asked, and never silently on reading.** The *mirrored file* is [D-211](#)'s **copy**: a medium points at a URL and holds a downloaded state beside it. ⚠️ *The owner did not recognise the term and does not know whether the legacy ever used it — so this adopts the legacy's rule on its merits, not on its record.* **Once on save, plus an explicit refresh, never a silent re-fetch on every read.** The silent version would make displaying a page reach out to a foreign server, so the page becomes as slow and as unreliable as the slowest source it mentions — and the copy would stop being the frozen snapshot [D-211](#) wants, since it would quietly track after all. **Refreshing is a choice between two things, not one action** — the owner: *the copy can only be renewed manually, when the user says: download another copy now. And then one might assign a version — either replace the old one, or download a new version, and perhaps add a description.* So a refresh either **replaces** the copy or **adds** a state beside it. ⚠️ **The description is the load-bearing part:** without it three files sit there and nobody knows why any of them exists, which is worse than having one. Versions of a copy are parts of the medium and live under `Compositions` ([D-232](#)), each carrying its file, its date and its description. | `agreed` | [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | from the legacy sweep |
| D-295 | 2026-08-23 | **Duplicating an attribute copies the definition, not the data. Copying values is a separate, named operation.** The legacy asked, when duplicating an attribute that already had data, whether to **copy the values**. The owner was sceptical and put his finger on it: *why should a second field with a different name hold the same data? Perhaps a transformation could run over it in the background — that would be possible.* ⚠️ **That is the tell: what he wants there is not a copy but a transformation**, which is the conflict resolver's world ([D-054](#)) and a model change with a history ([D-061](#)), not a checkbox on a duplicate. **So duplicating gives an empty attribute with the same shape** — and where values really are to come along, it is asked for as its own operation, with the transformation named. Hidden in a duplicate dialogue it would be a data migration nobody recorded. | `agreed` | [70 Migration](70-migration.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy sweep |

| D-296 | 2026-08-23 | **A machine change is recorded as the machine, never as a person.** Import, migration and pack updates write to the changelog too, and the owner offered two candidates: *either take the [person] or a system user — the system itself.* ⚠️ **The system user is the right one, and the reason is [D-061](#):** if the changelog is to read as a **history of intentions**, attributing a machine action to whichever administrator happened to be logged in makes the history **lie** — it says a person decided something nobody decided. **So: a reserved system identity as the changer, and the entry names which mechanism acted** — import, migration, pack update. That way *who changed this* stays answerable and *why* stays truthful. ⚠️ *The owner's alternative — the admin acts here — is recorded as rejected rather than unconsidered: it is tempting because there is always an admin around, and that is exactly what makes it misleading.* | `agreed` | [70 Migration](70-migration.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | from the legacy sweep |
| D-297 | 2026-08-23 | **Locale-dependent formatting follows the reader, not the installation.** Numbers, currency and dates read differently by locale — `1.234,56` against `1,234.56` — and the owner placed the answer where it belongs: *that reaches into the user preferences — what the user who is logged in, or not logged in on the front end, has set. Language, and with the language perhaps certain currency or date formats.* So **formatting is a property of the viewer**, resolved from their locale and falling back to the site's where none is known. It is a **converter** ([D-219](#)) that reads the locale, not a property of the type — `1234.56` is one number however it is written, which is [D-149](#)'s *notation is not structure* one more time. ⚠️ **This does not disturb [D-291](#):** the *stored* value is UTC regardless, and the *displayed timezone* stays the site's. Whether a logged-in reader may override the timezone as well is a separate question and not decided here. | `agreed` | [40 I18n](40-i18n.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | from the legacy sweep |

| D-298 | 2026-08-23 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-299](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **A list of tick boxes commits when it is left, not on every tick.** The legacy said it plainly: *tick freely — choices save when you leave this list.* ⚠️ **This is not an exception to [D-249](#)'s *settings apply immediately*, it is that rule applied correctly:** a single tick is not a finished thought. Turning five permissions into three means clicking through states nobody means, and under immediate saving each of those in-between states would become real — briefly forbidding something, briefly allowing something else, each one an event in the changelog. **The unit of intent is the list, not the box**, and that is what is saved. Applies to every multiple choice: allow-lists ([D-221](#)), the icon set, permitted MIME kinds ([D-287](#)). | **superseded by [D-299](#)** | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | from the legacy code sweep |

| D-299 | 2026-08-23 | **Commit-on-leave was a crutch, not a principle. Supersedes [D-298](#).** ⚠️ *I turned a workaround into a rule and dressed it in an argument — **a single tick is not a finished thought** — which sounds good and was not the owner's reason.* His reason: *every time I had a list like that with tick boxes, the page jumped down and up, because it saved each time. Then you click another list item and it is either pressed, or it reloads and is not pressed because it refreshed. That was horrid to use. Properly programmed I could imagine it working immediately as well; the last version simply did not.* **So the rule is the one already decided:** settings apply immediately ([D-249](#)), and the interface must not lose your place or jump ([D-283](#)). **Commit-on-leave stays available as a remedy** where the environment misbehaves — the same standing [D-249](#) gives the save button — but it is not the default and it is not a principle. ⚠️ **What survives of my argument is weaker than I made it:** ticking five boxes one at a time does write five changelog entries ([D-081](#)) where one intent was meant. That is worth handling — by writing one entry for a coherent change — but it does not by itself justify making people click *done*. ⚠️ **Recorded at length because it is exactly the failure the sweeps were warned about:** a crutch built for a defect that no longer exists, mistaken for a considered design. I set that trap for the agents and walked into it myself. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | supersedes [D-298](#) |

| D-300 | 2026-08-23 | **An allow-list is *one* value that happens to be a set. The group argument stands — with a third reason, and this one holds.** The owner, after [D-299](#) had downgraded it: *the many ticks are a **group**, and the group should only be saved together. That argument does hold.* ⚠️ **Third framing of the same small question, and the first that needs no rule bent:** not *a tick is not a finished thought* ([D-298](#), paternalistic), not *commit on leave* ([D-299](#), a crutch for a page that jumped) — but **the set is the value**. [D-249](#)'s *settings apply immediately* is untouched, because what applies immediately is **the value**, and the value is the whole set. So: **one write, one changelog entry** ([D-081](#)), and *does it save on every tick?* stops being a question — on a single tick the value has not finished changing. **[D-299](#) also stands**: the page must not jump and nothing may be lost ([D-283](#)); that was a real defect and its cure is not a commit button. ⚠️ **Generalised by the owner immediately afterwards, and his version is the better one:** *it is always about **groups** of decisions. The tick boxes mean which prefixes are allowed for a base unit, and those I want together. But there is also the case where I have to make a boolean decision and there is only one value — and then I cannot wait for the others.* **So the unit of saving is the group that belongs together, and a group may be large or may be one.** An allow-list is a group of many and is written as a whole; a switch is a group of **one**, waits for nobody and applies at once. ⚠️ *That makes the set no longer a special case, which is what my wording had made of it — the group is always the unit, and a single value is the same case in miniature. It also removes the last reason anyone would have to wait: nothing waits that need not wait.* ⚠️ *Kept as three entries rather than one edited row, because what changed each time was the **reason**, and the reasons are what a later reader needs — a rule kept for the wrong reason breaks the first time the wrong reason stops applying.* | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | completes [D-298](#) / [D-299](#) |

| D-301 | 2026-08-23 | **Where the data are must be visible in the product.** The owner: *it must always be clear where what is stored.* ⚠️ **The evidence is an incident from the same hour:** asked to back up the old data, I queried the database, got no output, read *no output* as *no data*, and told him his backup did not contain the tree. It did — 219 terms in `wtt_fs`, 21 in `wtt_case`, 2104 rows in `wp_wtt_node_presentation`. **If the person who wrote the concept cannot say where the data live, no user can.** So the product shows it: which tables, which taxonomies, how many rows, how much space — plainly, in the administration, not reconstructed from documentation. It costs almost nothing to display and it is the difference between deleting something confidently and deleting something blindly. | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | — |
| D-302 | 2026-08-23 | **Export, backup and restore belong to the product, not beside it. A backup replaces; a pack merges.** The owner: *export and backup of the data, and reading them back in again, should be a part of the project.* ⚠️ **Half of it already exists without being promised:** a **data pack** ([D-175](#)) **is** an export, and a backup is a pack containing everything. **But the difference is real and has to be named**, or one will quietly be used as the other. A **pack** is portable and **merges** into an installation that already has content — add, never alter ([D-177](#)); a **backup** is exact and **replaces**, carrying ids, versions and changelog so that afterwards the **same** state stands there and not a similar one. **Restoring is therefore not installing a pack** even though it looks alike from outside. This also generalises [D-289](#), which offered an export only at uninstall time: an export must be available **whenever**, because the moment you want one is rarely the moment you planned for. **Where it lands in a release is not decided here.** | `agreed` | [70 Migration](70-migration.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | generalises [D-289](#) |

| D-303 | 2026-08-23 | **The old data stay, and here is when they would be used.** The owner: *leave the data in, they do not bother me — but I also do not know when we would still need them.* An honest answer deserves better than *perhaps some day*, so the moments are named: **the importer** ([OQ-072](91-open-questions.md)), where 219 terms and 2104 presentation rows are the first real test of *guess the model or fill an existing one*; **checking [D-193](#)**, which the owner himself asked to be confirmed against real cases; **the standard tree as a data pack**, since the harvested fixture is from `0.0.479` while the running tree is newer, and a shipped seed should reflect what was actually built; and **speed against real numbers**, which [OQ-070](91-open-questions.md) and [D-228](#) both defer to *when a real table exists*. **A full backup was taken first** — `backups/wordpress-2026-08-23-vor-neustart.sql`, outside the repository because it holds users, addresses and password hashes. ⚠️ *If none of the four moments arrives, deleting later costs nothing; deleting now would remove the answer to a question not yet asked.* | `agreed` | [70 Migration](70-migration.md) | — |

| D-304 | 2026-08-23 | **Transforming the existing site content is the first thing that delivers value, not an afterthought.** The owner, at the end of the scenario check: *because we kept moving in loops, we never got round to actually transforming the data from the website.* ⚠️ **That reframes [D-173](#)**, which placed the importer as a boundary tool *for his own tables first and other people's second*. The evening's look at the real site shows what it is really for: **74 reusable Gutenberg blocks** that are records held as prose — `286 Testkonfiguration`, `386er Software`, `Bauteil Quellen`, `3D Druckparameter` — with free text where references belong. Until those are transformed, the product changes nothing about his day; the moment they are, he can ask questions he cannot ask today. **The `wtt_fs` tree is scaffold and is not the target.** ⚠️ *No release is decided here.* What is recorded is **why** the importer matters and **what** it should be aimed at, so that the question of when is answered against the right thing. And it names the yardstick the concept can be held to: *can it replace those 74 blocks, and let them answer what they cannot answer today?* | `agreed` | [70 Migration](70-migration.md), [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) | reframes [D-173](#) |

| D-305 | 2026-08-23 | **Versions of a board are modelled, not built. Closes [OQ-075](91-open-questions.md).** *Boards exist in different versions, which then have different parts lists* — raised twice by the owner and twice set aside. ⚠️ **The temptation is to build versioning into the engine.** It is not needed: `Platine` is the **design**, and its versions hang under it as a **composition** ([D-232](#)), each carrying its own parts list. *Which one is current* is an ordinary attribute on the design pointing at one of its versions. **Nothing new**, and it gains what a built-in mechanism would not have given: a version is a record like any other, so it can be compared ([D-207](#)), referred to, and carry its own attributes — a release date, a fault, a photograph. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | closes [OQ-075](91-open-questions.md) |
| D-306 | 2026-08-23 | **A conversion is a record, not a property of a unit. Extends [D-274](#). Closes [OQ-077](91-open-questions.md).** A tablespoon of flour is 10 g, of sugar 12 g, of honey 21 g: the factor belongs to the **pairing** of unit and substance, and [D-274](#) put it on the unit. **Modelled rather than mechanised:** a **conversion** is a record — from unit, to unit, for substance, factor, and where needed an offset. [D-274](#)'s factor on the unit is then the **special case in which the substance does not matter**, which is why inch and metre worked and the kitchen did not. ⚠️ *This is the third time this evening that a supposed gap turned out to be something the concept can already hold, if it is written as **data** instead of as machinery — see also [D-305](#) and [D-307](#).* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | closes [OQ-077](91-open-questions.md) |
| D-307 | 2026-08-23 | **A pattern book, because the concept can express more than it teaches. Closes [OQ-078](91-open-questions.md).** A relationship carrying its own values — supplier **plus** customer number **plus** since-when — is a **composition that aggregates**. Everything needed exists; what is missing is that anyone would find it. The move people reach for instead is a `Lieferant` attribute, then a second, then `Lieferant2`, which is how a model rots. **So: a short book of modelling moves**, half a dozen, each with one example — *a relationship with its own values becomes a node* · *a repeating group becomes a composition* · *the same thing in two roles* · *a version is a record* · *a conversion is a record*. ⚠️ **This is not documentation of the model, it is documentation of how to think in it** — the cheapest and most valuable thing that can be handed to a modeller, and the thing whose absence the previous round paid for. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | closes [OQ-078](91-open-questions.md) |
| D-308 | 2026-08-23 | **Where the shape stops fitting, said out loud. Closes [OQ-079](91-open-questions.md).** A hundred thousand sensor readings become several hundred thousand rows carrying two useful numbers each. **The answer is scope, not optimisation: this is a modeller, not a time-series store.** Thousands of records are unremarkable; hundreds of thousands are not what it is for. ⚠️ **It goes into [00 Vision and scope](00-vision-and-scope.md) rather than into a performance note**, because a limit discovered with a full table is a betrayal and a limit written down is an honest boundary. *For scale: the owner's own corpus is some 600 records across 23 tables — three orders of magnitude below the concern.* | `agreed` | [00 Vision and scope](00-vision-and-scope.md) | closes [OQ-079](91-open-questions.md) |
| D-309 | 2026-08-23 | **Reader-supplied parameters are parked; a page per record is Release 2. Closes [OQ-076](91-open-questions.md) and [OQ-080](91-open-questions.md).** *Four portions instead of two* needs an input from the **reader** at the moment of reading, which no part of the render context carries ([D-159](#), [D-217](#)). **Parked** ([D-271](#)) with its shape noted: an attribute may be declared **scalable**, and a block may then offer a control. It becomes wanted the first time a recipe is read by someone cooking for a different number of people. **And the page per record goes to Release 2**, downgraded after looking at the real site: the owner's pattern is data embedded in **his own posts**, which [D-206](#) already provides. A template and a route matter the day a catalogue of five hundred parts wants addresses — not before. | `agreed` | [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) | closes [OQ-076](91-open-questions.md), [OQ-080](91-open-questions.md) |

| D-310 | 2026-08-23 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-312](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **A use site is an attribute, and it may do everything the node may. [D-088](#) stands; the *never widen* sentence falls.** ⚠️ *Found while writing the specification: I had generalised [D-221](#)'s unit case into **restrictions narrow downwards and never widen**, without checking whether a rule already existed. [D-088](#) says the opposite and says it deliberately — **an override may narrow and widen, no monotonicity rule.*** The owner settled it in one line: *by use site you mean the attribute, right? That may do everything the node may.* **And the vocabulary is confirmed with it:** *attribute* and *use site* are the same relation seen from two sides — from the owning node it is an **attribute**, as the place where a shared node is used it is a **use site**. One thing, two names for two questions. ⚠️ **The consequence is worth naming because it is not obvious:** if an attribute may do everything the node may, then *only `Ohm` is permitted* on a type is a **default, not a guarantee**. Whoever needs something genuinely inviolable does not get it from a restriction — they get it from framework protection ([D-194](#)) or from a validator that is not overridable. That is not a defect but the consequence of the chain: **everything on it is a default**. One must simply know it before relying on a restriction. **What survives of my objection is a reporting requirement, not a prohibition:** a widening at a use site should be **visible**, since the complaint was never that it happens but that a promise made higher up quietly stops holding. | **superseded by [D-312](#)** | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | upholds [D-088](#); withdraws the *never widen* sentence of [D-221](#) |

| D-311 | 2026-08-23 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-405](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **What an ancestor declares mandatory stays mandatory for every descendant. A targeted exception to [D-310](#).** The owner arrived at it through the classic object-orientation case: *a bird can fly — but what about a penguin and an ostrich?* ⚠️ **The problem does not arise the way it does in OO**, because nodes have no methods: behaviour lives in renderers, converters and validators, which are **registered and chosen**, never inherited, so there is no inherited contract to break. *Can fly* is an **attribute** whose default a child overrides. **But his scepticism found the real cost of [D-310](#):** if everything inherited may be overridden anywhere, then *every bird has X* never holds — and that reliability is precisely what the OO restriction existed for. He raised interfaces as the historical answer and then set them aside himself: *no interfaces, that was only an example.* **So one axis is made strict and the rest stays free:** defaults, renderers, labels and permitted sets remain overridable ([D-310](#)); **mandatory may only be tightened downwards, never loosened.** ⚠️ **And the owner supplied the reason it is right, not merely useful:** *what we have is classification — a tree, and we configure data onto a model node; that is similar to taxonomy in biology.* In a biological taxonomy a characteristic stated at a level **holds for everything beneath it** — mammals have mammary glands, and no species argues. That is what makes a classification worth anything: one can say something about a whole group. **The penguin dissolves in the same thinking:** if penguins do not fly then *flies* was never a characteristic of birds — it belongs a level lower, or it is a **property with values** rather than a characteristic of the class. A taxonomist does not say *birds fly, except these*; he cuts the group differently. **So the strictness is not a restriction on the modeller — it is the rule that gives the tree its purpose.** A child may still make an optional attribute optional, and an attribute that does not apply is **moved down** ([D-155](#)) rather than refused. ⚠️ **Ersetzt** durch [D-405](#). | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | narrows [D-310](#) on one axis |

| D-312 | 2026-08-23 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-411](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **Bounding settings may only be tightened downwards; choosing settings are free. Supersedes [D-310](#) and the widening half of [D-088](#).** ⚠️ *[D-310](#) was written in haste an hour earlier — the owner's instinct that it cost too much turned out right.* **Widening is off the table for now.** ⚠️ **Die Aufteilung bounding/choosing ist ersetzt** durch [D-411](#); die Schlüsselliste gilt. | Kind | Examples | Direction | |---|---|---| | **bounding** — it limits what is possible | permitted set · range `min`/`max` · multiplicity · mandatory · **`hide`** · **`read_only`** | **narrower only** | | **choosing** — it picks within the bounds | default value · renderer · converter · labels · icon · order | **free** | **Why the strict half:** a restriction that may be reopened anywhere **says nothing when read**. To know what is allowed you would have to inspect every use site, and at five hundred attributes a model is then only locally readable. ⚠️ **And the deeper danger the owner's whale exposed:** whoever *can* widen, widens — instead of cutting the tree differently. The exception is cheaper than the rethink, and after a year the taxonomy is full of exceptions that were really signals that a group was cut wrong. **`hide` and `read_only` do not come from object orientation and obey the same rule anyway**, because the rule does not come from OO either: it comes from **what a reader can know about a type without looking at every use of it**. A child may hide what the parent shows, never reveal what the parent hid; may fix what the parent left editable, never unfix what the parent computed. **A default value is not a bound** but a choice inside the permitted set, so it stays freely overridable — as in OO. **The narrow-only start is deliberately the reversible direction** ([D-222](#)): it can be opened later, whereas a model that has widened everywhere never gets its guarantees back. Where more is genuinely needed, it is added **at the type**, where it is visible in one place. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | supersedes [D-310](#), [D-088](#) |

| D-313 | 2026-08-23 | **Implementation proceeds in self-contained packages: thin vertical slices, each ending in something the owner can operate, each followed by a list of what was assumed.** The owner proposed it out of a fear he named plainly: *the previous round started just as well, and then I had to spend hours explaining why a conclusion was nonetheless wrong.* His shape — **packages, not sprints**, self-contained but still surveyable, with the concept **unchanged** and only a grid laid over it. **Three additions, all aimed at that fear.** ⚠️ **(1) A package ends with something *he* can operate.** Not *the storage layer is done* — that cannot be checked, only believed — but *create a node, rename it, reload, it is still there*. **A package with no visible outcome is cut wrong.** ⚠️ **(2) Thin vertical slices, not horizontal layers** — table to screen for one small capability. A horizontal layer cannot be checked by a person, and that is exactly where the hours of explaining come from. ⚠️ **(3) After each package, a list of what I assumed that was not in the concept.** Gaps **will** be filled convincingly whether or not the filling is right; what can be prevented is filling them **silently**. Ten lines to read instead of a thousand, and what he does not sign off becomes a decision or comes out again. **And what actually changed since the previous round is not that the assistant reasons better** — it is that there is now something to point at: on 2026-08-23 he corrected two confident conclusions of mine, and both took minutes because a decision could be produced. **First cut: six packages** ([97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md)), ending where his real data first have somewhere to go. | `agreed` | [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | — |
| D-314 | 2026-08-23 | **The test for `locked` is whether a person can *check* what was built, not whether a person could build from it. And the core fits on one page.** ⚠️ *I had proposed the wrong criterion.* The owner read the finished specification and answered honestly: *neither I nor a human programmer could build from this — everything we described is in it, but you would have to jump around, and it is somewhat too large for a human mind. I believe an AI could.* ⚠️ **That is a risk, not a compliment.** If the only reader who can hold the model is the thing that also writes the code, **there is no independent check** — which is precisely how the previous round failed: confident production, nobody able to verify. **And it exposed a real defect in the document, not merely its size:** a good specification is **layered** — a small core a person keeps in their head, and beneath it what follows from it. Ours was complete but **flat**, 796 lines on one level, which is why one has to jump. **So: [the core on one page](10-domain-core.md#the-core-on-one-page)** — fourteen sentences from which everything else follows. Hold those, and any line of code can be judged without reading the rest; the 796 lines become what they should be, **a reference rather than required reading**. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | replaces the `locked` criterion |

| D-315 | 2026-08-24 | **A boolean is stored as an integer — and a missing row is not `false`.** The owner: *for me, storing bool as int would be fine.* So `value_int` holds `0` or `1`. ⚠️ **The trap is the third state.** At multiplicity `0..1` there are three: *yes*, *no*, and **not answered** — and whoever collapses the last two into `0` never gets them apart again. So: **a missing row means *not answered***, never *no*. That is also why the storage is a plain integer rather than a `NOT NULL` boolean column: absence has to remain expressible. | `agreed` | [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | — |
| D-316 | 2026-08-24 | **Long text is `MEDIUMTEXT` in `record_values`. And we store no binary data at all.** The owner asked where long texts go — *blobs?* ⚠️ **No: `BLOB` is bytes, `TEXT` is characters.** Source code has an encoding and must be searchable and collatable; it is text, not a heap of bytes. So `value_text` as `MEDIUMTEXT` (16 MB), in the **same** table as every other value — a separate table would be **a second place a value can live**, which this concept refuses everywhere. The usual objection, that it fattens the hot table, does not apply: InnoDB stores long `TEXT` **off-row** as long as nobody writes `SELECT *`. And should it ever hurt, moving it out is **a storage change only** — the model would not notice. ⚠️ **The rule that follows and is worth stating on its own: no binary data, ever.** Images, PDFs and drivers live in the WordPress media library ([D-229](#)); we hold the identifier as text. That keeps this a model database and stops it becoming a file store, which it cannot be and should not be. | `agreed` | [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | — |
| D-317 | 2026-08-24 | **Data can be translatable too. The gap: we had translation for labels and none for values.** Found by the owner: *we did provide translation for labels and so on, but not for data.* ⚠️ **He is right, and it is a real hole.** Labels carry `role` + `locale` and cover the **model** side — an attribute's name, its help text. A **record's content** had no locale at all, so on a bilingual site a recipe description, a part's long text or a code comment could exist only once. **Same construction one level down** ([D-261](#)): **the attribute declares whether its value is translatable**, and `record_values` gains a **`locale` column that is empty most of the time** — numbers, references and dates are language-neutral and never need it. Only where the attribute says so are there several rows. **Three consequences, settled with it:** the **fallback chain** applies as it does for labels ([D-020](#)) — a missing German text falls back rather than showing an empty field, which the owner chose deliberately over the stricter *leave it blank so it is noticed*; the **search column** ([D-167](#)) exists **per locale**, which costs nothing at this scale and is the only whole answer; and ⚠️ **a translatable attribute may not be `unique`** — a guarantee that depends on which language you happen to be speaking is not a guarantee, and no real case wants it: an article number is not translated, and a recipe name need not be unique. | `agreed` | [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [40 I18n](40-i18n.md) | closes a gap the owner found |
| D-318 | 2026-08-24 | **`textarea` is retired as a type. One `text` type; the differences are a validator and a renderer.** The owner pointed out that `textarea` exists in the seeded set — and looked at closely, `text` and `textarea` are not two types. ⚠️ **Same move as [D-245](#) yesterday:** *what a thing **is** is the type; what it **looks like** is the renderer.* A string is a string; whether it appears as a one-line field or as a box is **presentation**, and carrying it as two data types means two sets of renderers, converters and validators for one thing. **So the differences move to where they belong:** *single line, no breaks* is a **validator**; *box instead of field* and *source code with highlighting* are **renderers**; and the storage is `MEDIUMTEXT` in every case, which costs nothing while the field is short. ⚠️ **Source code is a composed type** — text **plus language** — because the language changes from record to record (one snippet PHP, the next JavaScript), so it is a **value** and not a renderer setting. Exactly the shape of `Resistance` = value plus tolerance ([D-150](#)). ⚠️ *Third time in two days that a legacy **type** turns out to be a **renderer**: `set`, `table`, and now `textarea`. The old project consistently mixed appearance with essence — which is no reproach; it is the commonest confusion there is.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | retires a seeded type |

| D-319 | 2026-08-24 | **A type earns its place through storage, rendering or ordering — never through validation alone.** ⚠️ *I had proposed the filter as **own validation or own rendering**, and it was wrong: by it, `phone`, `ip` and `mac` would each have become a type. The owner wanted them as **validators**, and that is right — **a validator is already a separate, attachable thing** ([V8](00-vision-and-scope.md)), so needing one creates no type.* **The workable filter has three grounds:** its own **storage** (`datetime`, `decimal`), its own **rendering** (`color`, `media`), or its own **ordering** (`version` — `1.10` comes after `1.9`, not before). ⚠️ *The third I had not thought of at all until the owner asked for `version`.* **So `phone`, `ip` and `mac` are `text` with a validator** — they look no different and sort no differently, they are only checked differently. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | corrects my own filter |
| D-320 | 2026-08-24 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-328](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **Four composed types are added: `range`, `link`, `period`, `address`.** Each is a case where two or more values belong together and nothing holds them together today. ⚠️ **Der validator-only-Vorschlag ist ersetzt** durch [D-328](#). | Type | Members | Why | |---|---|---| | **`range`** | from · to, both of one type | operating temperature −20…+70 °C, a voltage band, a tolerance band. ⚠️ **The most important of the four** — half a hardware specification is written in ranges, and today it would take two attributes that nothing keeps together | | ~~**`link`**~~ | | ⚠️ **withdrawn by [D-322](#)** — a resource already is one | | **`period`** | from · to, dates | a production run, an availability window | | **`address`** | street · postcode · town · country | suppliers and organisations | **All four follow [D-220](#):** the composed type is the unit of rendering, and its members stay individually searchable because they live inside the record by path ([D-232](#)). | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | — |
| D-321 | 2026-08-24 | **`version` is a type, and it earns that through ordering.** `1.10` comes **after** `1.9`; as text it would come before, and every sorted list would be quietly wrong. **Stored as text, compared part by part.** ⚠️ **It does not contradict [D-305](#).** That decision makes a *version of a thing* a **record** — a board version with its own parts list. This is the **number** on it, and a record may perfectly well carry one as a value. The two answer different questions: *which version is this?* is a record; *what is it called and how does it sort?* is this type. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | — |

| D-322 | 2026-08-24 | **`Medium` becomes `Resource`, the copy is optional, and `url` and `link` are dropped. An email stays its own type with a clickable renderer.** ⚠️ *I had argued for a separate `url` primitive beside the medium. The owner's thinking-aloud overturned it, and his own sentence is the reason: **in the old project downloading was optional** — so a medium without a copy **is already a link**, and a web page is a resource with MIME `text/html` that one rarely copies. Not two things; one thing with an empty field. He also named the instinct: **it goes against the grain to store URLs in two places.*** **And my objection — that every link then becomes a record — turns into an argument for him:** with a separate `url` value, *I would like a copy of that datasheet after all* is a **model change**, `url` becoming `Medium`, with data to move. With a resource from the start it is **filling one empty field**. **So `url` falls, and `link` from [D-320](#) falls with it** — a resource already has a base name and labels, so *URL plus caption* **is** one. ⚠️ **But the name had to change**: `Medium` says *file*, and this covers web pages too. A **resource** is something addressable that **may** have a local copy — URL always, copy optional, MIME detected, source and licence where they exist. **What stays separate, and the line that decides it:** > **A resource is something you *fetch*. An address is something you *contact*.** `info@reichelt.de` as a resource record, with a licence field and a MIME type, would be absurd: an email address is a **property of a person or company**, like a telephone number. So `email` remains its own type — text, validator, ⚠️ **and a renderer that draws it as a clickable link**, which the owner asked for explicitly. `mailto:` being a URL scheme is true and beside the point: the thing is an address, not a resource. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | renames [D-229](#); withdraws `url` and `link` from [D-320](#) |

| D-323 | 2026-08-24 | **It is called `Link` — a link with extras. Names [D-322](#).** ⚠️ *I had argued `Link` undersells the copy, and recommended `LinkedResource`. The owner ended it with one observation: **what if a resource is an image that only lives locally — that is also a link, to a local file.*** **He is right, and it dissolves the objection: there is no case that is not a link.** A remote PDF is a link outward, a mirrored one a link inward, an uploaded image was never anywhere else. What differs is **where the address points**, not whether there is one. My objection only held while I took the copy to be something other than an address; it is not. **So a `Link` carries one or more addresses** — a URL, an identifier in the media library, or both — plus MIME type, source and licence. ⚠️ **And the invariant that follows: a link with no address is not a link.** At least one must be present; which one is free. **`Resource` is dropped as a name** for the reason the owner first raised: in programming *resource* means REST resources, resource files, system resources and translation bundles, and is precise only because a glossary pins it — which is a weak kind of precision when the person who must recognise the word in two years already finds it vague. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | names [D-322](#) |

| D-324 | 2026-08-24 | **`tolerance` is a composed type of two magnitudes; symmetric is the case where they are equal.** The owner did not want two different tolerance constructs, and none is needed: `plus` and `minus`, both **quantities**, and *symmetric* is simply `plus == minus` — an equality, not a second shape. That covers the asymmetric case electronics needs, `+50 % / −20 %` on an electrolytic capacitor. **One converter, both directions** ([D-149](#)): it reads `±5 %`, `5 %` and `+50/−20 %`, and writes `±5 %` where the two are equal and `+50 % / −20 %` where they are not — one mapping with two writings on one side, not two converters. **Three validators, and the first is the real trap:** ⚠️ **both fields hold magnitudes, never signs.** Typing `−20` into the minus field means *20 downwards*, but would store *minus minus 20*; so negatives are refused **with the correction offered** ([V9](00-vision-and-scope.md)). **Both deviations must be of the same kind** — `+50 %` mixed with `−0.2 mm` is nonsense, and it is the sort that never announces itself and makes every calculation quietly wrong. Mandatory is the ordinary check. **In use one field is shown by default**; typing an asymmetric notation or opening the control makes it two ([D-223](#)). ⚠️ **And the `±` in front is notation, not structure** ([D-149](#)): the same value reads as `10 kΩ ±5 %` or as `9.5…10.5 kΩ`, depending on the renderer. **Distinct from `range`** ([D-320](#)), which is *from…to* **without a centre** — a tolerance yields a range by calculation, a range yields no nominal value back. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | — |
| D-325 | 2026-08-24 | **Percent is a unit, not a type — and an enumerable ratio is a constant, not a type. No `ratio` type.** `5 %` is a `quantity` with unit `%`, and it has siblings that convert into it: `‰`, `ppm`, `ppb`. ⚠️ **The rule worth keeping: what can be a unit becomes a unit.** Otherwise the type list grows by percent, hertz, ohm and gram and never stops. **On `ratio` the owner corrected my proposal and was right:** screen ratios — `4:3`, `5:4`, `16:10`, `16:9`, `21:9` — are a **fixed, countable vocabulary**, and by [D-184](#) small fixed lists are **nodes**, so they belong under `Constants`. Searching for other uses turns up two sorts: **countable** (aspect ratios, paper formats) and **arbitrary** (a gear ratio, a transformer's turns ratio, a scale `1:87`, a mixing ratio `1:10`). Only the second would justify a type, and **his data show no case for it** — a scale might appear in 3D printing, a mixing ratio in recipes, but both are speculation. So: none now, and `text` until something must be **computed** with. ⚠️ **And nothing is lost by that, because a constant may carry values:** the node `16:9` holds an attribute `ratio = 1.778`, so screens sort by aspect although the vocabulary is fixed — exactly as a base unit carries its conversion factor ([D-274](#)). **Fixed does not mean uncomputable.** | `agreed`, the `ratio` half revised by [D-326](#) | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | — |

| D-326 | 2026-08-24 | **`ratio` is a composed type after all — numerator and denominator — and the constants use it. Revises the `ratio` half of [D-325](#).** ⚠️ *I had refused it because I saw only the **arbitrary** case, which does not occur in his data. The owner turned it round with an argument I did not have: **the same type serves both.*** If the constant `16:9` carries its value **as a `ratio`** rather than as a `decimal`, the type exists anyway — and the arbitrary case (a gear ratio, a scale `1:87`, a mixing ratio `1:10`) then costs nothing more. **It earns its place twice over under [D-319](#):** its own **ordering** — `16:9` must sort by value, not alphabetically — and its own **rendering** — `16:9`, not `16/9` and not `1.778`. **Two rules it must carry, or it goes quietly wrong:** ⚠️ **never reduce automatically.** `1920:1080` **is** `16:9`, but `20:10` on a gear means *twenty teeth to ten*, and reducing it destroys the information. So the reduction is **offered, not performed** ([V9](00-vision-and-scope.md)). And **the denominator may not be zero** — obvious, and exactly where every sort dies. **[D-325](#)'s other half stands untouched:** percent is a unit, and what can be a unit becomes one. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | revises part of [D-325](#) |

| D-327 | 2026-08-24 | **The namespace is `Taxmod`, and the version starts at `0.0.1`.** `Taxmod\Core\…` and `Taxmod\WordPress\…`, mapped by Composer PSR-4 (**CD-3**) onto `src/Core/` and `src/WordPress/`. ⚠️ **The namespace is not decoration — it is the only way [D-170](#) can be enforced.** The core may call no WordPress function, and without a marker nobody notices a breach until, years later, somebody tries to reuse the core and finds it never worked. With it, **the place says which half a class belongs to**, and the two test runs of [D-169](#) become possible: the core tested **without** a WordPress bootstrap, so a WordPress call that drifts in fails on the first run. ⚠️ *Worth noting: `Taxmod` says what the thing **is** — a taxonomy modeller, [V1](00-vision-and-scope.md)'s own words — where the repository name `wp-taxonomy-tree` says what it **looks like**. The owner's reason is the better one: *we were never productive in the old project* — so there is no count to continue. | `agreed` | [00 Vision and scope](00-vision-and-scope.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | — |

| D-332 | 2026-08-24 | **A barcode is an additional renderer, not a type — and it is the first real use of the renderer list.** The owner: *one could make a barcode renderer and attach it in addition, since we said several renderers can be attached.
