Masking all but the beginning and end, or a telephone number too.* ⚠️ **And he named the condition that makes it worth anything:** *it would have to happen in such a way that the correct record is not visible in the source code either.* **So masking is applied where the value is rendered — on the server** ([D-254](#)) — and the page receives **only the masked form**. A mask done in the browser is decoration: the value is in the source, and anyone who looks sees it. **It is not storage.** The value is stored plainly like any other; what is masked is its **presentation**, which is why a converter is the right home and a *password type* is the wrong one. ⚠️ **This is not permission to keep secrets.** Passwords, keys and tokens are a liability, not a capability, and nothing here invites storing them; masking serves account numbers, telephone numbers and the like — data that legitimately exist and merely should not be on screen in full. | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | — |

If it returns, it returns as *reading a foreign format on import*, never as a place to keep things. | `agreed` | [95 Roadmap](95-roadmap.md) | — |

| D-335 | 2026-08-24 | **The type of [D-331](#) is called `markup`.** Text plus a declared interpretation — markdown, HTML, PHP, a recipe description with paragraphs and a link. **`markup` fits both ends**, where `code` reads wrong on prose and *rich text* reads wrong on a PHP snippet: source code is also only text with a markup that somebody interprets. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [01 Glossary](01-glossary.md) | names [D-331](#) |

| D-339 | 2026-08-24 | **`Identity` becomes a table: `identities(id)`, append-only, and `owner_id` becomes a real foreign key.** Model ids are allocated by its `AUTO_INCREMENT`; nodes and relations take the number and use it as their own primary key. ⚠️ **Why the counter it replaces was unsafe.** Package 1 allocated from a counter in `wp_options`, because [D-083](#)'s seven tables contain no identity table and two tables cannot share an `AUTO_INCREMENT`. **Its failure mode is not a crash but silence:** if the counter ever falls behind the data, ids are handed out twice, and since `owner_id` is one column over nodes *and* edges ([D-090](#)), a setting then attaches to the wrong object with no error anywhere. ⚠️ **And the realistic path there is a backup, not concurrency** — the counter sat in `wp_options` while the data sat in `taxmod_*`, two tables that can be restored separately. With `AUTO_INCREMENT` the allocation state **is** the data: every dump carries it, every restore keeps it consistent. **Three things are won, not one:** the state cannot desync; `owner_id` becomes the *real* foreign key [OQ-022](91-open-questions.md) option 3 asked for and could not have before, so an orphaned setting is structurally impossible; and **rows are never deleted**, so a purged object's number is never reissued — which matters because the changelog keeps the `owner_id` of things that are gone ([D-065](#)), and a reissued id would silently attach frozen history to a new object. ⚠️ **This is not a breach of [D-066](#).** That rule forbids a table **per model** — a schema that grows with content. `identities` is fixed infrastructure, and it is not a new idea either: **`Identity` is already a class in the concept**, the one `Setting`, `Label` and `ChangeLogItem` all hang off ([D-080](#)). It had no home in the tables; now it has one. **`version` deliberately stays on `nodes` and `relations`.** [D-080](#) places it on the identity, but moving it would make every read a join for no gain — the whole argument here is about the **id**. The owner named the trade himself: *the shared data then lie in two tables, but that is probably better for reading.* ⚠️ *If `creation_date` is ever materialised — the escape hatch [D-080](#) left open — `identities` is where it belongs: one row per object, node and edge alike.* **Seven tables become seven plus a base.** | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | completes [D-080](#), [D-090](#); refines [D-083](#); replaces Package 1 assumption 1 |

| D-340 | 2026-08-24 | **A migration may never reissue an id that was once handed out.** The owner, while the identity migration was being written: *note it for migration — ids already issued must not be given out again.* ⚠️ **The trap is that the naive migration looks correct.** Seeding a new counter from the highest **surviving** id is the obvious move and it is wrong, because the ids of **purged** objects leave no trace in any table — the very migration meant to stop reuse would cause it. So the rule is: **carry the high-water mark, never recompute it from what is left.** Where the old high-water mark is not recorded anywhere, the migration must refuse to run rather than guess. ⚠️ **And the damage is silent**, which is what makes this worth a decision of its own: `owner_id` is one column over nodes and edges ([D-090](#)), so a reissued number attaches settings, labels and frozen changelog history ([D-065](#)) to the wrong object, with no error at any point. **The same rule binds every future retirement of a column or a table:** copy first, drop after, and never let a derived value be rebuilt from a set that has had rows removed. *Seen once already in this repository:* `settings.key` was renamed to `setting_key` in the same upgrade and could be dropped outright **only** because nothing had ever written to it. | `agreed` | [70 Migration](70-migration.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | generalises [D-339](#) |

| D-341 | 2026-08-24 | **A reset is all or nothing, and it is command line only.** The owner, on wanting the id counter tidy again during development: *exactly, all or nothing.* ⚠️ **Resetting `identities` alone is the dangerous case**, and it is the one somebody would reach for: the counter looks untidy, the data look fine. But changelog rows, settings and labels survive pointing at numbers that are about to be handed out again — the silent mis-attachment [D-339](#) and [D-340](#) exist to prevent, produced by an act of tidying. **A reset is only safe when nothing survives that could name an old id**, so every table this plugin owns goes together or none does. ⚠️ **And it is not reachable from a browser** — not a hook, not an admin screen, not a REST route, and it refuses without an explicit `--yes`. Something that destroys a model must not be one mistyped URL away. *The legacy had the same tool behind a Development-mode toggle in the settings screen ([`_harvest/02`](_harvest/02-settings-page.md)); putting it on the command line instead removes the toggle, the screen and the chance of reaching it by accident.* | `agreed` | [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | follows [D-339](#), [D-340](#) |

| D-342 | 2026-08-24 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-564](#)** — *sein Satz darin gilt weiter; was fehlte, ist die Einschränkung, dass Konzeptänderungen möglich bleiben müssen und eine Prüfung den aktuellen Soll-Zustand bewacht.* **Every package adds to a regression net, and both runs are green before anything is committed.** The owner named the failure mode himself while Package 1 was being altered underneath: *can you somehow make sure that new implementations do not break old ones — that was another point in the old development.* ⚠️ **The answer is not care, it is a net**, because care is exactly what fails when a change is confidently wrong. **Two runs, and the split is [D-169](#)/[D-170](#) made mechanical:** a **core** run under PHPUnit that loads **no WordPress at all** — so a WordPress call drifting into the core fails on the first run rather than years later — and a **boundary** run against a real database for tables, foreign keys and migrations. ⚠️ *The first proof arrived the same hour:* the entire id allocation was replaced ([D-339](#)) and the boundary checks passed **unchanged**. And **fakes, not mocks**: a fake repository really stores and really moves subtrees, so a test asserts an outcome rather than that a method was called. | `SUPERSEDED` (durch [D-564](#)) | [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md), `CLAUDE.md` | applies [D-169](#), [D-170](#) |

| D-343 | 2026-08-24 | ⚠️ **Recorded in error as new — it was already [R19](30-renderer.md#r18r20--the-surfaces-are-renderers-all-the-way-up).** I searched [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) and not [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), and told the owner the split was nowhere. It had been an owner statement since 2026-08-22: *the modelling admin screen is split in two: the tree on the left, the settings of the selected node on the right.* **The entry is kept rather than removed**, because the log records what happened; what survives of it is the cross-reference below and [OQ-082](91-open-questions.md), which is genuinely open. **The screen is split: the tree on the left, the selected node’s properties on the right.**he owner, on seeing Package 2's provisional table: *we had the two-part split — tree on the left, properties of the selected node on the right; a table is fine for testing as long as we get the right thing afterwards.* ⚠️ **This was never written down.** [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) specifies the detail view thoroughly — what it contains and in which order ([U9](20-interaction.md)–[U14](20-interaction.md)) — but never says **where it sits**. Found by looking rather than assumed, which is the point of asking. **What follows from it, and is the reason it matters beyond layout:** the tree needs a **selection**, and today it has none. Selecting is then the ordinary way to look at a node — no separate *open* step, no second screen, no navigation away and back. That is also what makes [U10](20-interaction.md)'s loading argument work: the detail is fetched for **one** node, when it is chosen. ⚠️ *What is **not** decided here* — whether the split is resizable, whether the selection survives a reload or is addressable by URL, and what the right-hand side shows when nothing is selected. Those become [OQ-082](91-open-questions.md) rather than guesses. ⚠️ **What was already decided and belongs beside this:** the right-hand side is **not a hand-built screen**. [D-190](#) and [R20a](30-renderer.md#r20a--the-detail-view-is-not-a-special-screen) settle it as **a frame holding attributes rendered under the `edit` purpose** — nothing special-cased — for the reason that otherwise there are two ways to draw a field, *the official one and the one the admin screen was built with, and they drift*. **So this decision fixes where the frame sits; R20a fixes what it is made of.** | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | completes [U9](20-interaction.md), sits beside [D-190](#); raises [OQ-082](91-open-questions.md) |

| D-344 | 2026-08-24 | **The provisional admin screen is scaffolding. It is thrown away, not grown into the real one.** The owner, pausing after Package 2: *we had the node renderer in mind, so the tree is only a renderer too — I admit we never discussed it directly, I only mentioned it in passing.* ⚠️ **He had discussed it: it is [R18](30-renderer.md#r18r20--the-surfaces-are-renderers-all-the-way-up), his own statement from 2026-08-22** — *the tree view consists of nodes too, so a node can be drawn in the tree by a renderer* — with [R19](30-renderer.md) (split in two) and [R20](30-renderer.md) (the settings side is a page renderer) beside it, under one sentence: **a tree row is a rendered node; the settings page is a rendered page; nothing is drawn by hand anywhere.** ⚠️ **What Packages 1 and 2 built is drawn by hand**, and it is exactly the second way to draw that [R20a](30-renderer.md#r20a--the-detail-view-is-not-a-special-screen) warns about — *the official one, and the one the admin screen was built with, and they drift*. **So it is given a status rather than a quiet future:** it exists to make behaviour **checkable** before renderers exist, it draws **no value** and must never start to, and when the tree renderer arrives the scaffolding is **deleted** rather than refactored. ⚠️ **And it fixes an order that was not obvious:** the surfaces cannot be built first. A tree row is a rendered node, a node renderer needs settings and labels to render — so **Packages 3 to 5 come before the real surface**, and the useful thing to build now is behaviour, not screens. *That is also why the scaffolding is cheap to lose: every check written against it tests the **core**, which the real surface will use unchanged.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | applies [R18](30-renderer.md), [R20a](30-renderer.md); corrects the framing of [D-343](#) |

| D-345 | 2026-08-24 | **The scaffolding keeps getting just enough surface to be operated — sparingly.** The owner asked the sharp question: *you implement expand and collapse for the prototype and then throw it away to build it again with the tree renderer?* ⚠️ **Answered with a count rather than a claim:** the behaviour is `Tree::rowsUnder` with a collapsed set — 113 lines and 12 checks, which the tree renderer will call unchanged — and what is discarded is **31 lines of markup**. It exists only so that **he** can operate it, which is rule 1 of [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) and the actual protection against the previous round: *not the storage layer is done, which nobody can check, but you click it and it is still there.* **So the rule is kept and given a limit:** scaffolding is built **only where the owner would otherwise see nothing**, never for its own sake, and it never draws a value ([D-344](#)). | D-346 | 2026-08-24 | **Restoring from the trash is built, and the owner's collapse bug is kept as the evidence for [R18](30-renderer.md).** ⚠️ **Restoring was simply missing.** [D-123](#) makes deletion two stages and [D-172](#) says *undo reaches exactly as far as the trash* — but nothing put a node back, which makes the first stage a graveyard rather than a trash. **Where a node came from is read out of the changelog and nowhere else** ([D-065](#)), so no `parked_from` column duplicates a fact. Refused where the old place is gone or itself parked; **not a dead end**, since an ordinary move works on a parked node like on any other. ⚠️ **A consequence worth naming: the changelog's frozen state is now load-bearing.** Its text format was written in Package 1 as something to look at; a restore parses the path out of it, which makes the format a contract — read from the **last** `path=`, since a node may be named one. *If that ever needs to be robust, the state becomes structured; recorded so nobody rediscovers it by breaking it.* ⚠️ **And the evidence, found by the owner within hours:** collapsing worked in the tree and **not** in the trash — the same function, two call sites in the same screen, one of them forgotten. That is precisely what [R18](30-renderer.md) prevents by making a tree row a **rendered node**: *different behaviour in different places for the same functionality*, in his words. **The fix was not to pass the argument twice but to give both tables one path**, and the boundary run now guards it — because a rule that was demonstrated is worth more than a rule that was stated. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | completes [D-123](#), [D-172](#); evidence for [R18](30-renderer.md), [D-344](#) |

| D-347 | 2026-08-24 | **Restoring is undo, so a promoted child goes back with the node. [OQ-083](91-open-questions.md) closed by the owner's own reasoning:** *we intended undo — if restoring is not undo it may differ, but if it is, the child node has to come back.* ⚠️ **And it settles the premise too, from [D-172](#):** *undo is in scope, it is a step forward not a rewind, **and its reach is the trash**.* Reach **is** the trash means restoring **is** the undo, not a neighbour of it — so [D-127](#)'s *restore puts back the whole event* covers the promotion as well. Deleting a node and promoting its children was **one act, one button, one person**; undoing it undoes both. ⚠️ **What keeps this from becoming a rewind is [D-172](#)'s other half:** the return is written as a **new** change, an ordinary reparenting forwards. History is extended, never rewritten — otherwise the changelog stops being the migration script ([D-061](#)). ⚠️ **The hard case, and here the call is mine:** a child that has been **moved, renamed or deleted since**. Those are **left where they are and named** — bringing them back would overwrite a newer, deliberate decision with an older one, and silently. Untouched children return; changed ones are reported, in the shape [D-126](#) already prescribes for deletion — *not are you sure, but what exactly happens*. **A restore that says nothing while quietly relocating somebody's work is the worse failure than one that leaves two nodes out and says so.** ⚠️ **This cannot be built until [D-127](#)'s deletion event exists**, which it does not: parking writes one changelog line per node and nothing records that several things fell together. Today that is invisible because a parked subtree carries its own grouping in the paths; it becomes visible the moment something falls that is not a descendant. **So the event is the next piece of work on deletion**, before deletion is trusted with attributes. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | closes [OQ-083](91-open-questions.md); applies [D-172](#), [D-127](#), [D-126](#) |

| D-348 | 2026-08-24 | **Changes made in one act are written under one bracket. The changelog gains a change-group id, and [D-127](#)'s deletion event becomes one case of it.** The owner, generalising from the restore gap: *joint changes like deleting and reparenting here should have a bracket.* ⚠️ **He is naming the mechanism [D-127](#) needed and did not have.** That decision asked for a *deletion event* — *one deletion, with everything that fell with it* — which reads as a construct for deletions. It is not: **the same shape is wanted wherever one act writes several rows**, and deletion was merely where it was noticed first. | Act | What falls under one bracket | |---|---| | *delete only this node* ([U4](20-interaction.md)) | the parking **and** the promotion of every child | | *delete the branch* | the node and everything parked with it | | a move that runs through the conflict resolver ([D-162](#)) | the reparenting and every edge it rewrote | | installing or removing a pack ([D-175](#)) | every node, edge and setting it brought | | a migration ([D-210](#)) | every record it touched | **What it buys, and it is three things rather than one:** *undo* has something to take hold of — [D-347](#) restores an **act**, not a row; the history becomes **readable**, because *at 14:32 he deleted «Board»* beats twelve lines nobody can group by eye; and **[D-296](#)'s machine changes become answerable at the right size** — *the importer changed 4 000 values* is one entry with 4 000 under it, not 4 000 entries. ⚠️ **It is a column, not a table.** One id on the changelog row, allocated per act, null for a change that stands alone — because a second table would be a second place where history lives, and the changelog is the migration script ([D-061](#)). ⚠️ *And it is deliberately **not** a database transaction.* A transaction says *these writes succeeded together*; a bracket says *a person meant these as one thing*. The two often coincide and are different questions — a pack install may legitimately span several transactions and is still one act. ⚠️ **A shared version number was considered and does not work — the owner asked, and it was checked rather than defended.** Two version numbers exist and neither brackets. The **row version** on a node is per object, so two nodes in one act stand on different numbers — A goes 1→2 while B goes 5→6. The **model version** ([D-060](#)) answers *which shape was this written against*, which is a different question, and it is the near miss and therefore the trap: a rename does not change the shape, so two renames would share one number; [D-210](#) forbids re-stamping, so it does not advance on every act; and **a data change does not touch it at all** — the importer's four thousand values, which is the case the bracket helps most. **His instinct is right that a bracket is only a monotonic counter; it has to be its own rather than a borrowed one.** ⚠️ *And having one costs nothing:* **the bracket is the id of the act's first changelog row.** No second counter and no extra table — the first row carries its own number, and every later row of the same act carries it too. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | generalises [D-127](#); required by [D-347](#) |

| D-349 | 2026-08-24 | **The node counter moves on every write to its row, including a path rewritten because an ancestor moved. And it is a collision guard, not a version anyone can return to — so it stops being called one on any surface.** [OQ-084](91-open-questions.md) closed. ⚠️ **The owner's objection was right and my recommendation was wrong.** He said the numbers *feel unnatural, because an undo of a version goes back to a previous version*. Two things came out of taking that seriously. **First, a defect in my own proposal:** rule A — *rewriting a derived path is not a change to the node* — would have removed the guard that makes a stale form safe. **Demonstrated:** open a node, let somebody else move it, then rename from the old copy. Today it is refused, because the move raised the counter. Under A it would have gone through and **written the old path back**, silently undoing the move — because `save()` writes name and path together. **Second, my cost argument for B was simply false:** I claimed bumping a moved subtree is a write per row and collides with `CD-7`. It is not — `version = version + 1` rides in the **same** `UPDATE` as the path, so five hundred descendants cost no extra statement. **The rule is therefore B, and it is cheap.** ⚠️ **But the feeling he named is not answered by a rule, it is answered by a word.** `version` promises a state you can go back to; what it is, is *nobody changed this row since you read it*. `CD-9` says rename when the word lies. **What a person means by going back to a version now exists and has a name: the change group** ([D-348](#)). [D-172](#) already settles the mechanism — *the revert is a new change, never a rewind* — so returning to an earlier state means **replaying an act backwards**, and the number that identifies where you were is the **act**, not the row counter. ⚠️ *So the counter is not shown to a person as a version anywhere.* The scaffolding screen had a `Version` column, which is precisely where the unnaturalness came from — a person reads *Version 3* and expects a *Version 2* to return to. **It also fixes the inconsistency that started this:** promotion rewrote paths without bumping while restore bumped, so the same event moved the counter once and not the other time. ⚠️ **Refined an hour later, at the owner request:** the number goes back on the scaffolding screen — *put the version back in, I want to see it, there may be errors one can spot that way.* He is right, and it is the same hour proving it: **this defect was found by looking at those numbers.** So it is shown, and it is shown as what it is — a **write count**, not a version — because the promise in the word was the whole problem. A diagnostic that lies about what it counts is worse than no diagnostic. | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | closes [OQ-084](91-open-questions.md); applies [D-080](#), [D-172](#), [D-348](#), `CD-9` |

| D-350 | 2026-08-24 | **The scaffolding gets one raw data-entry surface, and it is the last one. [D-344](#) is bent here deliberately, not quietly.** ⚠️ **The conflict is real.** [D-344](#) says the scaffolding **draws no value and must never begin to**, because [R20a](30-renderer.md) warns that two ways to draw a field drift apart. But Package 6's own check is *enter something against a model and find it again*, and rule 1 of [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) says a package ends with something the owner can **operate**. **Without an entry field the package cannot be checked by a person at all** — only believed, which is the failure the whole plan exists to prevent. **So the line moves, with three fences:** it is **one raw text field per attribute**, no type-aware control, no formatting, no converter — it does not *render*, it accepts characters; it is **labelled as raw** on the screen, so nobody mistakes it for the editor; and it is **deleted, not evolved**, the moment the renderers arrive ([D-344](#) otherwise stands untouched). ⚠️ **What makes this defensible rather than a precedent:** the danger R20a names is *two ways to draw a field that drift*. A single unstyled `<input>` that knows nothing about the type is not a second way of drawing — it is the absence of drawing, and it cannot drift because it has no opinions to drift from. **The moment it grows one — a date picker, a number format, a chooser — it has become the second way and must go.** *Recorded because bending a rule silently is how the previous round's rules stopped meaning anything.* | `agreed` | [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | bends [D-344](#) once, with a stated limit |

| D-351 | 2026-08-25 | **Multiplicity is one key with four constants, not two number fields.** The owner: *there are only four multiplicities, we need four constants rather than two fields.* So `multiplicity_min` and `multiplicity_max` are replaced by **`multiplicity`** holding one of `0..1`, `1..1`, `0..*`, `1..*`. ⚠️ **Two integers could express `3..7`, which nothing in the model ever wanted — and a pair that contradicts itself, `min = 5` with `max = 2`.** One key with four values cannot be wrong, and a validator that has to catch what the type could have prevented is the weaker construction. ⚠️ **It changes what *narrower* means.** With two numbers it was arithmetic; with four constants it is **containment** — read each as the set of counts it allows and one narrows another when its set fits inside. **`0..1` and `1..*` are neither**, and that is the interesting half: going from one to the other trades *may be absent* for *may be several*, which is a **different** bound rather than a tighter one, so [D-312](#) refuses it just the same. That is expressed as `Narrowing::BySubset`, where the value type answers the question itself. ⚠️ **Multiplicity is also the one key that is edge-only** — a node describes a *thing* and a thing has no multiplicity; an edge describes a *use of a thing*, and a use does ([50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), *the asymmetry*). The owner, seeing it offered on a node: *multiplicity on a node makes no sense.* The core now **refuses** it there rather than merely hiding it, because a key unreachable through the screen is still reachable by an import or a data pack. ⚠️ *The owner's worry — that this changes storage — does not hold, and [D-232](#) is why: the branch decides where a value lives and multiplicity plays no part. Under the superseded [D-133](#) it would have been a real migration.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | replaces the multiplicity half of [D-086](#); raises [OQ-086](91-open-questions.md) |

| D-352 | 2026-08-25 | **Nine of the simple types get a renderer that comes in the box, and *which one is the default* is a fact the registry holds — not a convention a caller remembers.** [R14a](30-renderer.md#r14a--the-key-is-the-type-purpose-travels-in-the-context) says the registry key is the **type** and that *where several are eligible and nobody has chosen, one is marked default per type*. **Yesterday's contract could not express the second half**: it keyed on the renderer's **name** only, so a silent chain reached the fallback and every field in the product would have looked like a fault until somebody configured it by hand. That reason is right for a **value** and wrong for a **filter**, and the second case is the one [D-217](#) exists for: *declining `Search` is the mechanism behind «not searchable»* — a backward aggregate simply has no search capability and never appears in the filter. **Substituting the fallback there makes every attribute searchable again**, through a control that cannot search, and D-217's mechanism quietly stops existing. **So the registry answers `null`, which is a real answer, and the policy sits one level up in the descent:** a **value** must never silently disappear, so display and edit fall back — and the fallback **marks itself** in the markup, because reaching it at all means nobody chose and the type has no default ([R14b](30-renderer.md)); an **unanswerable filter** must never silently appear, so search yields nothing at all. ⚠️ *The test asserting the old behaviour was rewritten rather than kept, and the reason is written into it — a check that locks in the wrong answer is worse than no check, because the next person reads it as the decision.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | corrects the purpose-fallback half of the [D-217](#) implementation; applies [R14b](30-renderer.md) |

| D-354 | 2026-08-25 | **[D-350](#) is closed on its own terms: the raw entry field is deleted, not grown.** That decision bent [D-344](#) once — *one raw text field per attribute, no type-aware control, no formatting, no converter* — and named its own end: **deleted, not evolved, the moment the renderers arrive.** They have arrived, so it is gone, and every value on the modelling screen now goes through the same descent the real surface will use. ⚠️ **What went with it is the guessing.** The raw surface read a number as a number and everything else as text; each attribute now has a **type**, and the type reads its own characters back. **Nothing is coerced**: `abc` in an integer field is refused rather than stored as `0`, because a zero that arrived that way can never again be told apart from one somebody meant ([D-071](#)). ⚠️ **And this is not the converter.** [R36a](30-renderer.md#r36a--the-converter-removes-what-cannot-have-been-meant-the-validator-asks-about-the-rest) gives a converter the job of *removing what cannot have been meant* — `4k7` into `4700`, a locale's comma into a point. **None of that happens yet.** What exists is the type's own plain form, the one its control submits; converters will sit **in front** of it and nothing in it moves for them. *So `25.08.2026` in a date field is currently an error, and it is the honest kind: it will become a conversion, not a guess.* ⚠️ **What remains scaffolding is the frame, not the fields** — the row, the box and the diagnostics are still markup that gets thrown away with the rest of [D-344](#). | D-355 | 2026-08-25 | **The date granularity setting is called `date_precision`, it is a free key rather than a reserved one, and a time with no date is parked against the epoch — which is a compromise and is recorded as one.** [D-291](#) gives date, time and both together to **one** type with a precision setting; three things had to be settled to build it. **First, the name.** Not bare `precision`: [OQ-085](91-open-questions.md) is already asking how many places a **decimal** carries, and two meanings of one setting name is the kind of collision found by a wrong value on a screen rather than by a test (`CD-9`). **Second, free rather than engine-owned.** The eleven reserved keys are the ones an override may **narrow**, and there is nothing to narrow about *which parts of a timestamp to show*; reserving it would take a word out of every author's vocabulary for no gain ([D-084](#)). **Third, and this is the uncomfortable one: the column is a `datetime`, so a time of day has nowhere to sit without a date beside it.** The epoch is used, and the precision setting is what says the date part carries no meaning — but **a stored fact nobody meant is exactly what this model tries not to have**, so it is [OQ-088](91-open-questions.md) rather than a design. ⚠️ *Also settled by omission: silence means the whole timestamp. the validator asks about the rest.* | Comes from | Restricts | Expressed as | |---|---|---| | the **converter** in effect ([D-33b](#)/[R33b](30-renderer.md#r33b--several-are-eligible-exactly-one-is-in-effect)) | which **characters** may be entered | the control's accepted shape | | the **bounding settings** `range_min` / `range_max` ([D-312](#)) | which **values** are permitted | `min` · `max` on the control | | the **validator** | everything only answerable by asking | a **message**, and one per validator ([R36b](30-renderer.md#r36b--a-validator-message-is-a-label-and-there-is-one-per-validator)) | ⚠️ **A validator cannot own it, and not by preference:** it runs on the **value**, after conversion, when the characters no longer exist — and its answer is a sentence a person reads, not a restriction on a keystroke. ⚠️ **The owner's follow-up — *or both* — is right and is the more precise reading, so it is taken rather than argued with.** Both act on one entry, **in sequence and on different material**: the converter on the **characters** (`4k7` becomes `4700`), the validator on the **value** that comes out of it (*is 4700 permitted here?*). So a field is guarded twice and only the **pattern** has a single owner, because a pattern restricts characters and characters are the converter's material. *The mistake the first wording invited was reading «converter, not validator» as «the validator has no part in a field», which is false.* ⚠️ **And it leaves one edge genuinely open.** [R28](30-renderer.md#r28r32--the-rule-complete) wants a control to offer only real choices, which argues that a validator's rule should reach the control too — *must be one of these* ought to be a chooser rather than a message after the fact. **Nothing says how a validator surfaces into a control**, and the nearest thing, a permitted **set**, was deliberately left out of the engine keys because *narrower* has no meaning for a set yet ([D-312](#), and Package 4's own note). | D-358 | 2026-08-25 | **A renderer, a converter and a validator are always **chosen from what fits**, never named by hand — and that answers [OQ-090](91-open-questions.md) without needing nodes yet.** The owner, on being told the registry keys on a name: *of course the renderer should be picked — there are only certain ones for the current purpose, and how would the user know the name? Null now means *this subject has no simple type*, and what fits such a subject is a **structural** renderer (`handles() === []`): a form, a table, none of them built, so the honest answer is an empty list and the screen says so. ⚠️ **And on whether these things should be nodes** — the owner's own two reasons decide it, in opposite directions. | His reason for nodes | Does it need them? | |---|---| | *to show which ones exist* | **No.** That is the picker, and the picker needs no node — `eligibleFor()` is the list | | *to let a user create their own* | **Yes** — what a person creates in this model **is** a node. But he placed it on the parking lot himself: *that applies more to validators and converters, one could ship a construction kit, but that is future* | **So: names stay, and nodes arrive with the construction kit** — because a user-defined validator is a node by construction, and until one exists a node would buy only what the picker already gives. ⚠️ **The cost this decision first claimed does not exist, and the correction is [D-359](#).** It said `cols`, `rows` and `step` *belong to a renderer and have nowhere to live*. The owner pushed back — *the assignment we have through the registry; look at the concept again more closely* — and he was right on both counts. **There is no such thing as a homeless setting here:** a setting only one renderer reads is still a setting on the **node**, and the chain never asks who will read it — which is exactly how `range_min` already works. *The false premise was that a thing must **own** a setting in order to have one.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | answers [OQ-090](91-open-questions.md); applies [R14a](30-renderer.md), `CD-5`; extends [D-357](#) to how a rule is chosen |

| D-359 | 2026-08-25 | **`step` is an engine setting on the node, `range_step` — and the *homeless setting* that [D-358](#) worried about was never a thing. It says how the **control moves**, not what the model permits, and [D-312](#)'s narrowing rule protects what is *allowed*. **A granularity says nothing about what is allowed, so there is nothing to protect from being reopened.** ⚠️ **The second correction is the one worth keeping.** [D-358](#) closed [OQ-090](91-open-questions.md) while conceding a cost: *`cols`, `rows` and `step` belong to a renderer and have nowhere to live.* **That premise was wrong.** A setting only one renderer reads is an ordinary setting on the **node** — the chain resolves it key by key and never asks who the reader will be, which is precisely how `range_min` behaves for the two numeric renderers that read it and the many that do not. *The mistake was assuming a thing must **own** a setting in order to have one, and it is worth naming because it nearly bought a whole representation change — nodes for renderers — to solve a problem that did not exist.* ⚠️ *`cols` and `rows` stay **free** keys, and now for the right reason: no decision names them. R17 names the numeric triple; nothing names a text area's shape.* ⚠️ *And [R14](30-renderer.md#r12r14--name-in-renderer-out)'s second half deserves quoting where [D-358](#) argued from first principles: **every renderer records which node types it is responsible for when it registers — so the settings UI can offer a choice.** The picker is not an improvement on the concept; it is what R14 exists for.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | corrects [D-358](#); implements [R17](30-renderer.md); twelfth engine key, direction declared per Package 4's rule |

| D-361 | 2026-08-25 | **The field-rule panel has three sections, each with its own control — and the **default sits in the list**, marked as inherited, rather than being announced above it.** The owner, knowing full well the surface is still a prototype: *my expectation for setting renderer and converter would be a different one. Either you put it straight into that list, or it is shown above while the list is empty. **I would actually prefer it in the list, where the user can simply change it.*** ⚠️ **His preference is not a preference — [R33c](30-renderer.md#r33c--automatic-is-a-default-never-a-fact) requires it.** *Anything the system chooses on the user's behalf must be revocable at the use site*, and it has a second half: *an automatic choice must be **visible*** — the passage names his exact worry, a field showing red-blue-green with nothing anywhere saying where that came from. **A default announced in a sentence above an empty list is a fact you cannot act on; a default sitting in the list as its first entry is one you can change.** ⚠️ **One of his three sections gets a different control, and [R33b](30-renderer.md#r33b--several-are-eligible-exactly-one-is-in-effect) is explicit about why:** *this is the one place where the converter and the renderer list do **not** run parallel — [D-236](#) lets a node draw with several renderers at once; nothing extends that to converters, and nothing should be assumed from the symmetry.* | Section | Control | Because | |---|---|---| | **renderer** | select + **Add**, an **ordered** list, exactly one marked mandatory | [D-236](#), [R13a](30-renderer.md) — several are drawn, and the order is what you see | | **converter** | **one** choice, not a list to add to | [R33b](30-renderer.md) — several may be *eligible*, **exactly one is in effect** | | **validator** | select + **Add**, an unordered list | all of them apply, each with its own message ([R36b](30-renderer.md)) | ⚠️ **And one refinement his wording leaves out, which costs nothing and matters:** an entry that is only there because it was **inherited** has to look inherited until somebody touches it. Otherwise the chain goes invisible and [D-266](#)'s distinction loses its handle — *reset* means *inherit again* and *set to nothing* means *deliberately nothing here*, and neither can be offered on a row that does not say where it came from. The settings table's *from #id* column already exists for exactly this; the list inherits that job. ⚠️ **Confirmed the same afternoon by the owner's own mockup, and the mockup is what supplied the argument.** He sketched the four rules as numbered rows **inside** the settings table — *Renderer 1, Renderer 2, Converter 1, Validator 1*, each with a select, a `+` and a bin — then asked: *that is how I would picture it, which is also why the question — should we not rather **group** renderer, converter and validator into fields of their own and leave the node's settings by themselves?* **Yes, and his sketch shows why better than an argument could:** look at the *From* column on it — it says **here** on `Renderer 1` **and** on `Renderer 2`. **Two rows, one fact.** A key/value table cannot hold an ordered list without faking it, because the list is a single setting value ([D-362](#)) and every column that describes *where a value came from* then has to be repeated and mean nothing. ⚠️ **And the two are different kinds of thing, which is the reason underneath:** the engine's settings are **values** about a node — a number, a switch — each independent and each with its own origin; the field rules are **behaviour**, an ordered pipeline whose entries come from registries. *Putting a pipeline in a key/value row is what made his own sketch look wrong to him.* ⚠️ *One place the sketch and [R33b](30-renderer.md#r33b--several-are-eligible-exactly-one-is-in-effect) still disagree: it gives `Converter 1` a `+`. Several converters may be **eligible**; exactly one is **in effect**, so that section is a choice and not a list to grow — said once here rather than argued twice.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | shapes the panel [D-357](#) grouped; applies [R33b](30-renderer.md), [R33c](30-renderer.md), [R13a](30-renderer.md), [D-266](#) |

| D-362 | 2026-08-25 | **A rule list is stored as **one setting value holding the names in order** — one key, one row, and the list is the value. Answers what [OQ-089](91-open-questions.md) left open.** [D-357](#) settled *three keys for storage, one group on the surface* and left the shape of a list unanswered; [D-361](#)'s panel cannot be built without it. **The alternative was a key per position** — `renderer.0`, `renderer.1` — and it fails on three counts: it multiplies reserved names ([D-084](#)), it makes *reset* ambiguous (reset which position?), and a gap in the numbering becomes a state nobody meant. ⚠️ **One key keeps the property that made [D-093](#) worth having.** The chain resolves **key by key**, and a list stored as one value resolves as one unit — so a use site that overrides the renderer list overrides *that*, and the converter and validators keep arriving from the type. **A list spread over several keys would resolve position by position**, which is not a thing anybody wants to reason about: position 2 from the type, position 1 from the use site. ⚠️ **The list is one fact, and that is why replacing it wholesale is right rather than lossy.** *These are the renderers, in this order* is a single statement; there is no coherent meaning for *inherit the second entry and override the first*. ⚠️ **Direction: `Free`**, like the single-name `renderer` key it replaces — it is a **choosing** setting ([D-312](#)), and the one place a narrowing question arises is the validator list, where [D-311](#)'s logic suggests an ancestor's entry should stay. *That is left open deliberately: it is a question about validators, and there are none.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | answers the storage half of [OQ-089](91-open-questions.md); required by [D-361](#); preserves [D-093](#) |

| D-363 | 2026-08-25 | **A renderer learns about a node it is not drawing through the context, resolved before the descent — and the reference renderer is the first thing that needed it.** [D-105](#) says a reference is drawn as *the target's label plus a link, and nothing behind it*; [D-159](#) says a renderer reaches out to nothing. **Both hold only if somebody else resolves the label first**, so the descent does: every referenced node's label in **one** query for the whole form, then handed in. Asking per reference would be a query per row of a parts list, which is `CD-7`'s loop. ⚠️ **Deliberately one string, not a bag of everything a target has.** The summary renderer ([D-106](#)) will want more and the chooser more again — *that* is when the seam widens, because a bag would be fetched whether or not anybody drew it. ⚠️ **A constant is a reference, and [D-232](#) is where that comes from rather than a name.** The branch decides where a value lives: for `Constants` the value **is** a reference to a node, so the type to draw is `node_ref` whatever the constant is called — nothing is read off its name, which would be the special-casing `CD-9` forbids. *That is what finally removed «no renderer» from every constant on the screen.* A `Model` target is a reference to a **record**, has no simple type, and stays undrawn until the summary renderer exists. ⚠️ **The renderer supports `display` only, and that is the honest half.** Changing a reference means **picking** a node — the chooser, decided ([D-244](#)) and not built — so the edit purpose is declined and the descent falls back with its fault marker. *A box somebody could type an id into would have hidden the gap and invited exactly the wrong gesture.* ⚠️ **And the link is not built, for a reason that is now a pattern rather than an excuse.** A URL belongs to a surface and the core has none (`CD-1`). **How a core renderer emits something a surface must complete is the same question as how it emits a word** — [OQ-087](91-open-questions.md), which now has two symptoms and is one question. ⚠️ *A dangling reference — a label that never arrived — is drawn as a marked fault rather than as its id, because a bare number on screen is the sort of thing that gets copied into a spreadsheet as if it meant something.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | implements [D-105](#) within [D-159](#); applies [D-232](#); sharpens [OQ-087](91-open-questions.md) |

| D-364 | 2026-08-25 | **A free setting key is configuration about the *model*, never a place to keep data about the *thing* — and it is therefore not offered as an authoring gesture. If a record answers it, it is an attribute.** The owner, seeing a *declare a key of your own* box appear beside the real settings: *the idea of making your own keys is nice, but that is what we have attributes using data types for.* ⚠️ **He is right, and the implementation was the evidence.** The free-key row is the **only** one the panel cannot draw — nothing knows its type, so it prints characters and says so. *A thing that can only ever be a raw text box in a typed system is a thing people will put data in*, and it would be data with no type, no renderer, no label and no validation, sitting where none of those can reach it. **The whole of Package 7 was spent removing exactly that kind of box** ([D-354](#)). | The test | Answer | |---|---| | **does a record answer it?** | yes → an **attribute**: a type, a renderer, labels, validation, multiplicity | | | no → a **setting**: configuration about the model, resolved along the chain | ⚠️ **The mechanism stays, and it has a real job.** `cols` and `rows` are free keys today and correctly so — no instance has *rows*; it is how one renderer draws. **What changes is who writes them: whoever writes renderers, not whoever models.** So `declareFree()` remains in the core as the guard on inventing a name ([D-084](#) still owns the reserved list, and an import or a data pack may still bring one), and the screen no longer invites it. *A free key already stored keeps its row — what is gone is the invitation, not the capability.* ⚠️ *And this is the second thing the same afternoon that the panel's own undrawable row exposed. The first was [D-361](#)'s default-in-the-list; both were found by the owner looking at a screen rather than at a document, which is what the prototype is for.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | narrows the use of [D-084](#)'s free keys; applies the test of [D-232](#) |

| D-365 | 2026-08-25 | **Renderer, converter and validator are three orthogonal jobs assigned per *data type* — and [the per-type table](30-renderer.md#the-legacy-per-type-assignment-as-exported) is where that is written down. A validator serves `int`, never `spinner`.** The owner, spelling it out because he thought it was already clear: *an int converter is something different from an int validator or renderer. The renderer says **how it is displayed**. The converter says **convert the output to binary**. The validator checks **whether the input is correct**. So each has its job and is not an `int_spinner`. And there is currently only **one** validator for `int`, not one for the spinner. I thought that was clear because we have the table — at least I created it initially: data type, assignment, settings, renderer …* ⚠️ **The table exists and I had read past it.** It sits in [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md#the-legacy-per-type-assignment-as-exported), read out of the exported tree, and its columns are exactly the ones he names: | Type | Renderer | Converter | Default validator | |---|---|---|---| | `int` | `IntRenderer` | `roman`, from `arabic` · `binary` · `octal` · `hex` | `integer_shape` | | `char` | `CharRenderer` | glyph · ascii · unicode · the numeral systems | `char_shape` | | `text` · `textarea` · `bool` | … | — | **none by design** | ⚠️ **The consequence that matters for the code: the assignment axis is the *type*, for all three.** A renderer declares which types it draws (`handles()`, [R14](30-renderer.md#r12r14--name-in-renderer-out)); a converter and a validator will declare theirs the same way. **Nothing hangs off a renderer.** *There is one `integer_shape` because there is one `int` — and three ways to draw an integer do not make three validators, which is the same argument [R15](30-renderer.md#r15--variant-and-circumstance-are-different-axes) makes against eighteen classes.* ⚠️ **And his wording corrects [D-356](#) on a real point.** That decision said the accepted-input shape comes from the converter — true, and only half of it. *Convert the **output** to binary* is the other direction, and it is the one the table actually records: `int` carries the **numeral systems**. **A converter is a notation mapping, and it runs both ways** — [R35a](30-renderer.md#r35a--notation-is-not-structure) (*notation is not structure*) and [R36c](30-renderer.md#r36c--invertibility-decides-only-whether-a-form-can-be-written-into) (*invertibility decides only whether a form can be **written into***). So `42` shows as `XLII` regardless; whether `XLII` may be **typed** is what invertibility answers. *D-356 stands and was describing the input half as if it were the whole.* ⚠️ *One column the table does not have, flagged in the concept and still unread: [D-240](#) puts a **sample value** on every data type so a preview is never empty — and the owner's own memory of the old project is that **`int` already has the value forty-two**. A harvest row worth opening that sheet for.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | names the per-type table as the authority; refines [D-356](#); shapes how [D-357](#)'s three keys are registered |

| D-366 | 2026-08-25 | **A container renderer lays out parts the descent already drew; it does not draw them. And the first one built — the form renderer — found that three of the four things I expected to be missing were decided all along.** The owner cut off a drift and named the way out: *should we tackle the renderers now, so we see what is a missing concept and what is owed to the prototype?* **That is what happened, and the tally is the point of writing this down.** | What a container needs | Where it turned out to live | |---|---| | the **order** of its members | ⚠️ **fully decided** — [R75](30-renderer.md#the-default-layout)/[D-118](#): read-only, ordinary fields, booleans collected, multi-valued last, *reasons included so nobody improves it away* — **and [D-082](#)'s `position` orders within a group**, so a careful author is not rearranged | | which members to **leave out** | decided — `hide` ([R11](30-renderer.md)), with [R75](30-renderer.md#r75s-level-dependency)'s level dependency for read-only | | the **frame** around it | decided by scope: [R20a](30-renderer.md#r20a--the-detail-view-is-not-a-special-screen)'s order is the **node detail view**, and [D-098](#) says a form is *a node's attributes stacked* — no frame of its own | | each member's **label** | ⚠️ **the one real gap.** It should read in the **`form` role** ([D-196](#) seeds one by that name) and the label has to arrive in the context the way a reference's does ([D-363](#)). Until then it shows the edge's internal name — the same honesty the label chain itself ends on ([D-020](#)) | ⚠️ **The genuine finding is structural and it came from designing rather than from reading.** [R46](30-renderer.md#r46r47--a-container-renderer-is-the-same-recursion) says *every cell goes back to the registry*, and [D-159](#) says a renderer reaches out to nothing. **Both hold only if the container is not what asks.** So the **descent** resolves and draws the members and the container **regroups** them — a layout over finished parts, not a driver. *That keeps R46's recursion without handing a renderer a repository, and it is the same seam [D-363](#) opened for a reference's label: what a renderer needs about anything other than its own value arrives in the context.* ⚠️ **One consequence worth naming: `eligibleFor()` on a thing stopped being empty.** A node with no simple type wants a **structural** renderer — `handles() === []`, chosen for what a subject *is* — and until this class existed there was none, so *nothing offered* was the honest answer and is no longer. Two core checks and one boundary check asserted the old truth and were rewritten with the reason in them. ⚠️ *And one small gap left open rather than filled: [R75](30-renderer.md#how-it-fits-with-the-authors-own-ordering)'s grouping is a **configured default** ([D-032](#)) — *a renderer or a use site may switch to strict author order* — and **no setting key is named for that switch**. Not invented here; it is one line of [OQ](91-open-questions.md) the day somebody wants it.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [97 Implementation plan](97-implementation-plan.md) | implements [D-098](#), [R75](30-renderer.md)/[D-118](#) within [D-159](#); extends [D-363](#)'s seam |

| D-367 | 2026-08-25 | **Two renderers, not one: the tree renderer walks the hierarchy, the node renderer draws the node — and the point of the split is that the node renderer can be swapped.** [OQ-091](91-open-questions.md)'s first half, answered by the owner: *a renderer for the tree node is justified, so that **every node is rendered the same way**. The tree renderer only sees to it that the tree is walked and builds the hierarchy, and the node is then rendered by the node renderer. You can do that in the tree too — but perhaps we want to render the nodes differently for the **tree chooser**, and then we only need to swap the node renderer.* ⚠️ **That is [R46](30-renderer.md#r46r47--a-container-renderer-is-the-same-recursion) again, and it is the third time the same shape has answered a question today:** a container walks and places, a cell draws — the form renderer over its fields ([D-366](#)), the descent over a reference's label ([D-363](#)), and now the tree over its nodes. **The tree renderer is a container; the node renderer is the cell.** ⚠️ **It also settles what a *tree row* is: not a thing.** There is no tree-row renderer. A row is the node renderer's output, placed by the tree renderer at its depth — which is why [D-251](#) said *the node renderer in the tree* rather than naming a second renderer, and why [D-256](#)'s *node renderer and page renderer are one* holds without an exception for the tree. ⚠️ **And the swap is the argument, not a nicety.** The modelling tree, the tree chooser ([D-107](#), [D-244](#)) and the trash all walk the same hierarchy and want the node drawn differently — a chooser row has no delete button and may show what is pickable. **One walker, several cells** is the only arrangement in which those three cannot drift apart, which is the fault [D-346](#) demonstrated the hard way: *collapsing worked in the tree and not in the trash, the same function, two call sites, one of them forgotten.* ⚠️ **The owner also corrected an assumption I had made and stated: the row carries its buttons.** I had built the row as *display only* on the reasoning that acts belong to the surface; his mockup shows buttons in the row, and he confirmed it. **So a node rendering includes what can be done to the node** — which raises a question this decision does not answer: **which** buttons, and how the renderer knows what is possible for that node. *A protected node cannot be deleted ([D-194](#)); the last child has no «down» ([D-050](#)). That is knowledge the renderer must be handed rather than fetch (D-159), and nothing says its shape yet.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | answers the first half of [OQ-091](91-open-questions.md); applies [R18](30-renderer.md), [R46](30-renderer.md), [D-251](#), [D-256](#) |

| D-368 | 2026-08-25 | **The glyphs on the controls, named — and *icon* stops meaning two things.** The owner opened it: *we should also discuss the icons you should use for the buttons. Delete we already had, the wastebasket. We have `+` `−`, we have collapse and expand, up and down arrow.* ⚠️ **I first read that as *`+` and `−` are collapse and expand* and changed the triangles; he corrected it — *the triangles were fine*.** He was listing **which controls need a glyph**, not reassigning them. | Act | Glyph | |---|---| | delete | 🗑 the wastebasket | | expand · collapse | ▸ ▾ the triangles, unchanged | | move up · down | ↑ ↓ | | add an entry to a rule list | `+`, with `−` or the bin to take one away ([D-361](#)) | ⚠️ **These are not *icons* in the sense this concept already uses the word, and closing that now is worth an hour later.** [D-251](#)'s **node icon** is **data**: a setting on the node, resolved along the chain, a glyph from the installation's allow-list, chosen by a model author. **A button glyph is interface**: code, at the boundary, chosen by nobody using the product. *Same word, two things — the pair [D-252](#) had to separate for icon and symbol, for the same reason: they sit next to each other on the screen.* ⚠️ *And because the triangles stay, `+` has exactly one meaning — **add an entry** — which is the tidier outcome. My misreading would have given one glyph two jobs a few centimetres apart on the same screen.* ⚠️ *The wastebasket carries a meaning from elsewhere in this product and must not lose it: the **trash is a place** ([D-123](#)), not only an act. A row's bin **parks** a node there; purging is a second, separate act, and one glyph must not come to mean both.* | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | separates interface glyphs from [D-251](#)'s node icon |

| D-369 | 2026-08-25 | **The modelling tree shows a node's own **name**, not a label in a role — which closes [OQ-091](91-open-questions.md)'s second half and removes a query with it.** Asked which of the five seeded roles ([D-196](#)) a tree row should read in, the owner: *there I would take the node name.* ⚠️ **It is the right answer for a reason worth writing down: the modelling tree is where the model is worked **on**, and a model's names are what one works **with**.** A label is what a **reader** should see; a name is what an **author** manipulates. Reading labels there would mean the same node answers to two words depending on the locale of whoever is modelling — and the modeller is the one person who needs the stable one. ⚠️ **It also removes the friction rather than answering it.** The cell now needs nothing handed in: `cellsFor()` no longer resolves labels, `Surroundings` lost the field that carried them, and a name can never read as nothing because a node cannot exist without one ([D-022](#)). *A field nothing uses is a field somebody will use wrongly; the chooser can ask for one back when it has a reason.* ⚠️ **What stays open is the other half of the same question, and it is narrower now:** which role a **reading** surface asks for — a form field, a table column, a block on a page. [D-366](#) named it as the form renderer's one real gap and it is still that. *`AR-2` is untouched either way: user-visible names of user-created things go through labels, and the modelling tree is not a reading surface.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [40 I18n](40-i18n.md) | closes the tree half of [OQ-091](91-open-questions.md); leaves the reading-surface half open |

| D-370 | 2026-08-25 | **A control that cannot be used now is **greyed**, not absent — [U8](20-interaction.md#u8--what-cannot-apply-is-not-shown) is reversed.** The owner: *I would prefer functions that are unavailable to be only greyed out — then the row of buttons looks the same everywhere.* ⚠️ **U8 said the opposite in as many words** — *the last child has no down arrow … not greyed out, **absent*** — so this is a reversal and not a reading. **Its premise was the legacy's row and no longer holds:** U8 argues absence *is what keeps a **seven-control** row readable at all*, and today's row has **four**. At four in fixed places the eye learns a position; with omission the bin slides left on every row that cannot move up, so the most destructive control is the one that never sits still. *That is a worse cost than a greyed slot.* ⚠️ **And it makes the concept consistent with itself, which is the part worth keeping.** [R28–R32](30-renderer.md#r28r32--the-rule-complete)'s own table already answers this question with **disabled** and **preselected, greyed** — never *gone* — so the toolbar rule was the outlier rather than the norm. *[D-050](#) still stands underneath both: do not **ask** what cannot matter. A greyed control asks nothing.* ⚠️ **What does not change: who decides.** Availability rests on things a renderer must not fetch — whether a node is protected ([D-194](#)), whether it is the last child — so the **boundary** decides and states it on the control ([R1](30-renderer.md), [D-159](#)). *A disabled button submits nothing, so keeping it is a question of layout and never of safety.* | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md), [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | reverses [U8](20-interaction.md); aligns with [R28–R32](30-renderer.md); keeps [D-050](#) |

| D-371 | 2026-08-25 | **An attribute can be removed at last, and what was missing was storage: `relations.parked_by_group_id`.** The owner: *I cannot delete attributes.* ⚠️ **He was right and it had been open since Package 3**, whose own write-up says so — *removing an attribute … deleting a node does not yet park the attributes pointing at it*. **The blocker was never the rule but the table.** [D-123](#) makes deletion two stages, [D-125](#) parks a referencing edge, [D-128](#) describes a **parked attribute** on screen — *hidden by default in its owning node, one «show deleted» toggle away, greyed, labelled «deleted with X», with a restore action* — and `relations` had **no column** in which any of that could be recorded. ⚠️ **Why a column here and not on a node.** Package 1 settled that *parking is a move, not a flag*: a node's **position** is the mark, so a `deleted` column would be the same fact twice. **An edge has no position in the tree**, so there is no first truth to duplicate and a column is the only place the fact can live. *The rule was never «no flags»; it was «no second copy».* ⚠️ **And it holds the *act*, not a boolean.** `parked_by_group_id` is the **change group** ([D-348](#)) that parked it, which is what gives [D-128](#)'s *deleted with «X»* something to name — one column carrying two facts, neither of them a copy, and a bare flag would have needed a second column beside it. ⚠️ **Nothing else is touched, and that is the decision rather than the shortcut.** The settings written at the use site stay; so do the values records hold through it. [D-156](#) observes that *the trash preserves exactly the information the decision needs* — throwing the overrides away at parking time would discard what a restore has to put back. **Purging is a separate act and is not built.** ⚠️ *Removal is **idempotent**: once parked an edge leaves the live list, so a second click — a double tap, a back button, a stale form — would otherwise be refused with «not one this node owns», which is both wrong and confusing. It **is** owned; it is already gone.* ⚠️ *And an **inherited** attribute cannot be removed from a descendant: it belongs to the ancestor that declared it, and removing it lower down would be [D-155](#)'s «moved down» by another route — a different act, not this button.* ⚠️ *Found while building, by a boundary check: `$wpdb->prepare()` turns a **null** into an empty string, which a `bigint` column stores as **0** — so restoring an attribute left it parked by act zero. The `NULL` is now literal in the statement. A core test could not have caught it; only a real database could.* | `agreed` | [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md), [50 Persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md), [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | unblocks what Package 3 left open; implements [D-128](#) within [D-123](#); schema version 7 |

| D-372 | 2026-08-25 | ⚠️ **Überholt durch [D-373](#) und [D-377](#)** — was hier steht, gilt **nicht mehr unverändert**; die geltende Fassung steht dort. *Welcher Teil fiel, sagt die Zeile weiter unten.* **Prefixes and base units are seeded under `Constants` — `Gramm` and not `Kilogramm`, and a prefix is stored as a power of ten.** The owner asked for the branch and answered the question the concept could not: *yes, take the legacy list — but only Gramm, not Kilogramm.* ⚠️ **The shape was decided and the members were not.** [D-274](#) settles that *each unit carries its factor to the parent's reference unit*, and where a factor is not enough an **offset** — °C is Kelvin **shifted**, not scaled. **Which units exist was nowhere in `NewConcept/`**; it was read out of the legacy tree and confirmed name by name, because legacy is a quarry and never a source (`PR-1`). ⚠️ **`Gramm` is the owner's call and it is the right one for a reason worth keeping:** the prefix axis needs an **unprefixed** base, and `Kilogramm` already contains one — prefixing it would produce *kilo-kilogramm*. *The SI base unit is the kilogram and the modelling base cannot be. Where the physics and the model disagree, the model wins, because the model is what has to compose.* ⚠️ **A prefix is stored as an exponent, and that is a finding rather than a preference.** The obvious reading of D-274 is *a prefix has a factor* — but the column is `decimal(30,10)`: ten decimal places and twenty integer ones, so **neither 10⁻²⁴ nor 10²⁴ fits**. | D-391 | 2026-08-26 | **The screen's paint moves out of PHP into `assets/admin.css`, enqueued on that screen's own hook.** The owner asked the question that settles it: *do you put that in a taxmod css or do you hard-code it?* **Hard-coded**, in a `<style>` block — fine while it was three lines for the tree, and by then forty: a stylesheet living inside a string, every quote escaped, no editor help, re-sent on every page load instead of cached. ⚠️ **What moves and what does not.** A **renderer keeps its inline layout** — that a form is a flex row, that a cell is a column — because that shape **is** the drawing and has to hold on any surface; *a renderer that hard-coded a height would carry it into every block on the front end.* What belongs in the file is what is true of **this screen**: sizes, weights, colours, the paint on a switch. ⚠️ **Enqueued on `admin_print_styles-{hook}`**, so it is not loaded onto every page in `wp-admin` — which is why the wiring sits beside `add_menu_page()` rather than with the other hooks: the hook name is what that call returns. **Versioned with the plugin** so a released change reaches a browser holding the old file, and **`dashicons` is a declared dependency**, because the icon chooser draws glyphs as *characters* and needs the font — an admin page loads it anyway, and relying on that would be relying on an accident. ⚠️ *It also carried the fix the owner asked for in the same breath — `the borders in the fields are a bit big for my taste, which makes them quite tall … or margin?` **Both**: WordPress gives an admin `input` a `min-height` of 30px, 3px of padding and a 1px margin, so a row of five fields carries five gaps too.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md) | — |

| D-394 | 2026-08-26 | **A decimal loses its storage padding at the boundary, on read — not in the core, and not in the check that had enshrined it.** `decimal(30,10)` returns `2.7000000000`, and it was reaching the screen: the owner's `2.7 kΩ` drew as `2.7000000000 k Ω`. ⚠️ **The first fix was in the wrong layer and a test refused it.** Trimming inside `TypedValue::ofDecimal()` broke *`2.50` must not come back as `2.5`* ([D-057](#)) — a value a person typed is theirs, and the core has no business rounding it. **The padding is a storage artefact, so it comes off where storage ends**: `StoredDecimal`, in the repository. ⚠️ **The package check had `2.5000000000` written down as the expected value** — the bug recorded as a rule, which is the failure mode `PR-9` exists to make visible and which a green run cannot show. *Four checks were rewritten rather than loosened this day; each had been honest when written.* ⚠️ *That `2.50` reads back as `2.5` remains true and is a different question — trailing zeros are significant digits in engineering and nothing stores that intent yet.* | `agreed` | [50 WordPress persistence](50-wordpress-persistence.md) | narrows [D-057](#) |

The renderer control has to say *the stored name cannot be used here*, and that is its own row. ⚠️ *The same experiment produced a second surprise worth writing down: he had also set `persistent = 0` on `Base units`, and because that node is the **target** of the `einheit` attribute, the chain carried it down to the edge — so the unit could no longer be **stored** either. **A setting put on a constant reaches every attribute that points at it.** That is the chain working, and it is a coherent thing to say by accident.* | `agreed` | [30 Renderer](30-renderer.md), [10 Domain core](10-domain-core.md) | applies [D-363](#), [D-105](#), [D-159](#), [R14a](30-renderer.md), [R14b](30-renderer.md) |

| D-416 | 2026-08-26 | **One place assembles the address a person returns to after an act, and it carries every circumstance.** ⚠️ **Corrected within the hour, because the first fix fixed nothing.** The owner reported it again after it: *the show hidden setting still disappears when choosing a function at the node.* **`backTo()` read `$_GET` — and an act is a POST to `admin-post.php`, which has no query string at all.** So the redirect was carefully carrying values that no longer existed by the time it was built. *The circumstances now travel in the **form** as hidden fields ({@see submissionFor()}), and one reader takes them from the post or the query string, whichever this request has.* ⚠️ *Never `$_REQUEST` for that: it folds cookies in, and a circumstance that could arrive from a cookie would be one that outlives the visit it belongs to.* ⚠️ **He reported the same symptom twice and was right both times about different causes** — the missing redirect, then the missing fields. *A fix that leaves the symptom is not half a fix; the hidden toggle carried the folded set **and** the mode; the redirect after an act carried **neither** — only the page, the message and the node. *So every button on a node silently unfolded the tree and left the hidden mode, and only the second of those was visible enough to report.* ⚠️ **The fix is not a fourth careful copy.** `backTo()` is the one assembler, so a fourth circumstance means changing one line — *and the three copies that disagreed were each careful.* ⚠️ **`$extra` is applied last so a caller can override rather than only add.** The hidden toggle is the caller that needs it: passing `null` replaces the ambient value and `array_filter` drops the key, which is what *stop showing hidden nodes* has to mean in a URL. ⚠️ *And it is the price of the right decision, not a symptom of a wrong one. A circumstance is **not** model state ([D-389](#), [D-396](#)) — it is who is looking, right now — so it rides in the query string precisely in order not to be stored. **Carrying it is the cost of not storing it**, and this is where that cost is paid.* | `agreed` | [20 Interaction](20-interaction.md) | applies [D-389](#), [D-396](#) |
| D-417 | 2026-08-26 | **The message after an act is an overlay, not a band at the top — and that is what was making the page jump.** The owner, after three rounds on the tree: *now I see why the whole page jumps — because of the message output at the top. | D-419 | 2026-08-26 | **The saved scroll offset is read once, before anything can scroll — and the `#fragment` is gone.
